**D-171 (2026-09-27) — v0.4.0, stage 1: the corrupt-input case (owner instruction), five inputs each a frozen real record with one stated edit between valid records, synthetic-derived under the D-096 precedent (owner approval of the five inputs, 2026-09-27); its outcomes counted in the vocabulary of D-142 and D-144; the reference reader and adapter changed so that the control passes it. The case count in prose, the version constants, the site, the release notes and the Batch G re-runs are untouched (owner instruction).** Read first: `docs/handoff/v0.4.0-plan.md`, whose sections 5 and 10 list the inputs and settle decisions 2 to 4. The inputs (`tools/derive_corrupt_inputs.py` writes them to `derived/corrupt-input/`, six files, each with a provenance record naming its records, the `expected.json` each record's values were frozen in, the SHA-256 of the provider file behind them, and the edit): the corrupt record is always 69999's first record (stable tier); the valid neighbours are 25544's first record (stable) and 20453 from the decaying snapshot (live tier, its values frozen in `bstar-and-derivative-forms/expected.json`), five-digit records with a full international designator, so that no neighbour trips a parser for a reason unrelated to the corrupt input (an Alpha-5 field or a blank designator would have stopped the naive parser before the corruption). (1) Line 1's checksum digit changed from 6 to 1. (2) Line 2's character at column 30, the fourth digit of the eccentricity, removed: the line is 68 characters and every column after the cut moves one to the left. The first build read the plan's words, "cut to 68 characters", as a cut at the end, which takes only the checksum digit and leaves every value readable; the owner had the cut moved mid-line, since a short line that still parses to correct values is not the garbage case, and input 1 already covers the acceptance of a malformed line. (3) The 0 in column 26 of line 1, the second fractional digit of the epoch, replaced by the letter O; a letter counts 0 in the checksum, as the 0 did, so the checksum stays valid and cannot catch it. (4) Line 2 removed, line 1 followed by the next set's name line. (5) A CSV that ends inside its last row's MEAN_MOTION_DOT value (-0.00000023 cut to -0.000000; MEAN_MOTION_DDOT and the line ending missing), and a JSON array with its closing bracket and line ending removed. Every TLE input sits between the two neighbours; the CSV holds them before the cut row, the JSON holds all three complete. Everything is rendered by the corpus from values in the public `expected.json` files, and the tool reads no provider file, so a public clone rebuilds the same bytes [tested: a test rebuilds them, and passes in a public export]. The TLE lines come from the renderer that reproduces CelesTrak's lines (304 of 304), checked against each record's frozen TLE fields, so for these three records the unedited lines equal CelesTrak's own: their values were already public in `expected.json`, and the files are assembled by the corpus, not provider responses. The CSV and JSON follow CelesTrak's column order and CRLF endings with the frozen values in plain decimal notation. The grading (kind `corrupt-input`, `Runner.run_corrupt_input`, six new checks): each file gives two items. The input's own check covers the corrupt record: refused with a reason passes, a record built from it with other values fails as misidentified, and a silent drop fails; read as the record it carries, it fails, except on input 1, where it is reported as "validates no checksum" (decision 2). `corrupt-input-neighbours-load` covers the valid records: they must load with their values. A parser that raises on a file has refused it as a whole, with the exception as its reason: for the two cut files that passes, the complete records given up reported as info (decision 3); for a TLE file it fails the neighbours item, since the valid sets are lost. The JSON, which has no corrupt record, passes on a whole-file refusal or on a refusal reported with a reason, and fails on silent partial loading. The counts ride on the input's item in the existing keys (expected, returned, loaded, misidentified, refused, refused_matched, dropped and the rest), and no new report shape was added. The shifted column stays held (decision 4). The control needed changes: as committed, the reference adapter failed its own case on four items. It raised on the whole file for line 2 cut short and for the letter, so the neighbours were given up. It passed over the orphan line 1 with nothing said. And its reader built a record from the cut CSV row, reading MEAN_MOTION_DOT as 0 and MEAN_MOTION_DDOT as None [tested, with HEAD's reader and adapter against the new runner]. Changed in `gpconf/reference.py`: a non-numeric TLE field is now a ValueError naming the field; a letter used to raise `decimal.InvalidOperation`, which the runner reported as an internal error of the corpus, a gap in D-117. `read_csv_text` now rejects a row whose field count differs from the header's. Rebuilding every case's `expected.json` with these readers changed no value and no hash [tested: the diff held only `generated_at` and check and coverage texts that the frozen files predate; those files were restored]. Changed in `gpconf/adapters/reference.py`: `tle_sets` reads TLE sets one at a time. It refuses a set the strict reader rejects, with the reader's reason, and refuses an orphan line 1 or line 2, so the sets after it load; a valid file gives exactly `read_tle_text`'s records [tested]. The adapter does not declare the refusal channel, since tests use its output as plain records; `tle_sets` sits after the class so that the lines `docs/ADAPTERS.md` quotes did not move. The three built-in adapters. The reference passes: input 1 is reported, inputs 2 to 4 are refused with the neighbours loaded, and both cut files are refused whole by the reader, the complete records given up counted. The naive adapter fails three items: it builds a record from line 2 one character short (mean motion 1.623733631, eccentricity 0.148004 and mean anomaly 45.3718, each read one column off), it raises on the whole file at the letter (float()) so the neighbours are lost, and it passes over the orphan line 1 without reporting a refusal; input 1 is reported, and both cut files are refused whole, which passes. python-sgp4 2.27 through its built-in adapter: a finding was recorded, kept in `docs/handoff/corrupt-input/` under the D-134 convention. For that adapter the orphan's outcome is the adapter's own line pairing, not the library's, since python-sgp4 reads one set at a time. Other changes. `tools/cases.py` gains the six checks and the case, placed after `tle-writer-alpha5` so that no case number moves (the writer's "must stay last" comment now says what it means). `tools/make_expected.py` gains `build_corrupt_input`, and `meta_for` keeps a provenance record's own provenance. `tools/make_manifest.py` gains the interpretation text, with no library findings. `manifest.json`, `MANIFEST.md` and `fixtures/corrupt-input/` were regenerated; the other `case.md` files came out byte-identical. CI's two offline runs gain the case. Tests: `tests/test_corrupt_input.py`, 20 tests: the files match their provenance and are rebuilt byte for byte; each TLE input is one stated edit of the clean rendering; the control and the naive adapter are checked per file; each rule is checked with a parser written for it; the reader changes are checked. In `test_runner`, the offline list and the naive adapter's must-fail list gain the case. `test_action` and `test_install_layout` now expect the count lines the Action and the CLI print from the manifest: 5 of 18 cases exercised offline, 18 cases. Suites: 277 tests OK (7 skipped) under Python 3.14; 277 OK (4 skipped) in the virtual environment with python-sgp4; 275 OK (21 skipped) in a public export [tested, exit codes checked]. Not done here: the count change across the README, the site, `CITATION.cff` and the release notes; FAILURES.md, which would gain the case's row; the version constants; the Batch G re-runs. Alternatives: line 2 cut at its end, the plan's wording read literally and the first build (replaced on the owner's instruction, as above); the reference adapter refusing whole TLE files (rejected: the plan requires the neighbours to load); declaring the refusal channel in the reference adapter (not needed: it refuses rather than drops).

**D-172 (2026-09-27) — A local path removed from three test-shim docstrings, and a test that fails on any absolute home path in an exported file (owner instruction); the fix is forward only, since the path stays in what v0.3.0 published.** Line 2 of the docstrings of `tests/adapters/reference.py`, `naive.py` and `sgp4_adapter.py` should read `--adapter tests.adapters.reference:Parser` (and `naive:Parser`, `sgp4_adapter:Parser`). Instead it read `--adapter tests.adapters.`, then the absolute path of the owner's working copy (the macOS home folder of the owner's user name and the Dropbox folders under it down to the repository), then `referencearser`. The cause: the files were written through a zsh shell in commit 5d67cd8 (v0.3.0 stage 2, D-151), where `$name:Parser` applies the `:P` (realpath) modifier to `$name` and leaves `arser`. `CLAUDE.md` already says to write `${a}:Parser` and never `$a:Parser`; the rule existed and did not stop this, so the guard is now a test. In `tests/test_export.py`, `test_no_absolute_home_path_in_any_exported_file` reads every file of a fresh export, whatever its type, for an absolute home path of any user, in the three shapes: macOS and Linux (`/U…/<name>`, `/h…/<name>`, root's home), and Windows with a drive letter and backslashes, escaped backslashes or forward slashes. A path joined to a word character, as in a URL or a relative path, does not count. `test_the_home_path_guard_knows_every_shape` checks the pattern against a sample of each shape, including the one this leak had (after a dot), and against four strings it must not take for home paths. The one allowed path is the fixture of `tests/test_install_layout.py`, the macOS home of a user named u, allowed by file and path together. The pattern is spelt with character classes and the samples are assembled at run time, so that the test file does not match itself. Before the docstrings were fixed the test failed on exactly the three shims and nothing else [tested]. The fix is not retroactive. The path is in the v0.3.0 tag (tag object f3a7387 on 627f66f), and so in the source archives GitHub serves for the v0.3.0 release and in the v0.3.0 Zenodo archive, record 22986178, whose one file, `gp-omm-conformance-v0.3.0.zip`, is GitHub's archive of the tag [inferred from its source; the tag's tree carries the three files, tested with `git grep` on the tag]. It is also in public main's history. None of these is rewritten: tags and published history are not rewritten here, and the files of a published Zenodo version cannot be replaced. The PyPI files do not carry it: the wheel and the sdist of gpconf 0.3.0 contain no `tests/` files and no such path [tested on the local copies, whose SHA-256 equal PyPI's, 74611c02… and 21983403…]; the owner's instruction had listed them among the places, and the check found them clean. v0.2.1 and every earlier tag predate 5d67cd8 and are clean [tested: `git grep` on v0.2.1]. What leaked is a user name and a folder layout, nothing sensitive: no credential, no token, no data. So the answer is to fix forward, in the next export and in the next release's archives, rather than yank or re-release v0.3.0. A yank would withdraw a release whose files on PyPI are clean, and would remove nothing from the tag or from Zenodo. Alternatives: yanking or re-releasing v0.3.0 (rejected, as above); rewriting the tag or main's history (rejected: published history is not rewritten); a check for this one path, or for the owner's user name alone (rejected: the guard has to catch the class, any user's home path in any of the three shapes); relying on the `CLAUDE.md` rule alone (rejected: it existed).

**D-173 (2026-09-27) — v0.4.0, stage 2: the case count from seventeen to eighteen in the corpus (owner instruction), after an inventory of every place the number appears. On the owner's decisions: the lines about `pip install` wait for the release, the version stays 0.3.0, and the site and the profile README wait.** The inventory was re-derived rather than taken from the v0.4.0 plan's section 7, which predates the profile README. Every tracked text file of the private corpus, of the site at origin/main and of the profile README was scanned for the count in words and digits, for "n of 17" and "17 case(s)", and for the counts that move with it (the cases that run offline, the cases that need provider data, each adapter's failing cases); then each line was read in its context. Outside the repositories: the GitHub descriptions and topics of the three repositories and the corpus wiki's Home page carry no count; the Zenodo v0.3.0 description (655 characters) carries none either, which closes the plan's open check [tested: the record's metadata]; PyPI's 0.3.0 page and the v0.3.0 release notes are historical and are replaced by the next release. Changed now, as statements about the repository: in the README, the repository table's "17 cases" becomes 18, the clone control's "16 cases pass" becomes 17, and the case table's heading becomes eighteen, with a `corrupt-input` row. python-sgp4's "7 of 17" becomes "8 of 18", and a "What breaks" item 11 covers reading a corrupt record as if it were whole. In `docs/ADAPTERS.md`, the cases that run from shipped files go from four to five. The README's Status paragraph stays the 0.3.0 statement, with one sentence added: the eighteenth case is on main, unreleased, and until the release `pip install gpconf` gives the seventeen. The CHANGELOG gains an `[Unreleased]` entry, as the writer case had in Phase 2. Waiting for the release: the README's pip block ("the 4 cases whose files ship in the package run; 13 say they need provider data", "then all seventeen"), since `pip install gpconf` installs 0.3.0 and its seventeen cases until 0.4.0 is on PyPI. The site also waits: the home, findings and migration pages, `failures.json` from `docs/FAILURES.md` at the release tag, and `tests/test_site.py`'s literal. So does the profile README's sentence on the corpus. Unchanged, since each was true when written: the CHANGELOG entries for 0.1.0 to 0.3.0; this log; `AUDIT.md`; `docs/PLAN.md`; `docs/upstream/pr170-test-comment.md`; the README's PR #170 sentence (the sixteen cases of the time); the published results in the `harnesses/` READMEs (n of 17, runs of 2026-09-24). On the site: the hand-run table and its test pins, the timeline's past releases, and the about and activity pages' sixteen. In the profile: "all seventeen cases on 2026-09-24". And the private notes. Regenerated, not edited: the three adapters' reports (`tools/_out`, not tracked, with the adapter names used before). From them, `docs/FAILURES.md`, which gains the case's row: the reference passes, and the naive adapter and python-sgp4 have three failing items each. Over the eighteen cases the naive adapter fails 15 and python-sgp4 8, with 24 failing items (21 before), and the reference passes 17 with the SATCAT data check not exercised [tested: the runs]. Also regenerated: `manifest.json`, `MANIFEST.md` and the case's `case.md`. The tests that pin the count moved in stage 1 (D-171). Published on the owner's decision: python-sgp4's three failing items in `docs/FAILURES.md`, and a library-findings line in the case's `case.md` that cites python-sgp4 #116 and its README section "Double-checking your TLE lines". The behaviour is documented and by design, so publishing it does not put out a finding ahead of the maintainer, the case the D-134 convention guards against. D-171's "a finding was recorded" stands as written. The line was checked before it was written: `sgp4.io.twoline2rv` raises ValueError on both corrupt lines ("TLE format error" on the short line 2), and the cut CSV row's TypeError comes from `omm.initialize`, from float() of the missing MEAN_MOTION_DDOT [tested]. The `case.md` heading for this case no longer cites `docs/CROSSCHECK.md`, which holds none of it. The version constants stay at 0.3.0 (owner); none of them carries the count. Suites: 279 tests OK (7 skipped) under Python 3.14; 279 OK (4 skipped) in the virtual environment with python-sgp4; 277 OK (21 skipped) in a fresh public export, where a reference run with no provider data reads "18 case(s): 5 pass … 13 need fetched data" [tested, exit codes checked]. Alternatives: changing the pip lines now (rejected by the owner: they would be wrong for pip users until the release); rewriting the Status paragraph for the unreleased state (rejected: it describes the release a reader installs); leaving the changelog for the release (rejected: the Unreleased precedent); keeping python-sgp4's items out of `docs/FAILURES.md` (rejected by the owner: the behaviour is documented upstream).

**D-174 (2026-09-27) — v0.4.0 stage 3, library 1 of 8: PyEphem 4.2.1 re-run against the eighteen cases (owner instruction), with the refusal channel adopted in the `pyephem` preset, and compared item by item with round one's run (D-128), which was reproduced first; a finding recorded and kept privately at `docs/handoff/batch-g/pyephem/round-2/`, outside the export allowlist; nothing filed.** Versions, by the owner's rule for this round (the current release where a library has released since round one, round one's version where it has not): PyEphem's latest release is still 4.2.1 of 2026-02-28, the version of round one [tested: PyPI, 2026-09-27]. The baseline: round one's run was reproduced before anything was compared, from a detached worktree at private 30f8d3b with round one's adapter and the provider files git does not track copied in; its case table and every fail and pass-tolerance line are identical to the recorded run [tested]. The harness change, corpus-side and itemised because it changes how outcomes are counted, not what PyEphem does: the preset's list now begins with the refusal declaration, and a set `readtle()` raises on comes back as a refusal carrying the library's message where it used to be dropped (D-144; section 8 of the v0.4.0 plan gives the channel to the harnesses of the four libraries that raise on a malformed line: libsgp4, astroz, Gpredict and PyEphem). The line pairing is unchanged, so a line 1 with no line 2 after it still never reaches the library. The preset was run as shipped and with the change, so that the harness change and the new case can be told apart. Result: 7 of 18 cases fail (round one: 6 of 17). Against round one, item by item: of its 170 items, 169 are unchanged in status and text; `satcat-legacy-below-70000` is not exercised (D-129); six empty-answer items are new passes (D-143), which move three cases from skip to pass; the new case adds ten items and is the seventh failing case; nothing else moved to or from fail. The refusal channel moved two items of the new case from fail to pass and changed two texts, one item's and the headline of the gate over this month's launches, which now says "dropped silently" where it said "refusals not reported by this adapter"; no item of the seventeen earlier cases moved, since `readtle()` refuses no set there. Observed on the way, about the corpus: the new case's `corrupt-input-neighbours-load` item compares the records around the corrupt one with the frozen expected values as the values check does, so a parser that reads those records imprecisely everywhere fails it in every file whatever it does with the corrupt input, and the report counts the records as misidentified. PyEphem reads both neighbours in every file exactly as it reads them without the corrupt set [tested], so the item counts round one's single-precision and second-derivative results a second time. A change is proposed in the handoff note and not made here; the row's count does not depend on it [tested with a probe]. `docs/ADAPTERS.md` quotes the preset whole, so its excerpt and the paragraph on refusals are updated, and a new paragraph says the line pairing is the adapter's own. The changelog's Unreleased section says what changes for a user of the preset. `tests/test_presets.py` gains two tests, run where PyEphem is installed: a refused set comes back as a refusal carrying exactly what `readtle()` raised, and a line 1 with no line 2 after it is passed over; both fail against the preset as it was. Suites: 281 tests OK with PyEphem installed (6 skipped), with python-sgp4 installed (6 skipped) and with neither (9 skipped) [tested, exit codes checked]. Alternatives: running the preset as shipped only (rejected: the channel is the plan's, and two of the new case's items would fail on a reason the harness threw away); making the preset refuse an orphan line itself, as the reference adapter does (rejected: the refusal would be the harness's, credited to PyEphem); comparing with the recorded run without reproducing it (rejected: the recorded run lists only its failing and within-tolerance lines, so an item that moved between pass, skip and not exercised would not show). **Filed 2026-09-28T00:07:08Z from the owner's account on the owner's go as https://github.com/brandon-rhodes/pyephem/issues/297**, with the reference `docs/LETTER-IN-FIELD.md` (D-193) (link added under the D-134 convention; the text as the owner approved it, paragraphs unwrapped for GitHub's line-break rendering).

**D-175 (2026-09-27) — The corrupt-input case compares every record a parser returns from an input with the same parser's reading of the same records unedited, three files that now ship beside the inputs, instead of with the frozen expected values (owner instruction, after the first library run of round two, D-174); PyEphem's row re-stated under it.** What the item was measuring: `corrupt-input-neighbours-load` was meant to ask whether a corrupt input leaves the records around it alone, the question of resynchronisation. It compared each of those records with the frozen expected values as the values check compares them, binary floats within a relative 1e-12 and every core field present, so it asked two questions at once: whether the corrupt input changed the records, and whether the parser reads these records exactly at all. For a parser that fails the values check on 25544 or 20453 anywhere, the second question answered for the first: the item failed in all four TLE files whatever the parser did with the corrupt input, and the report counted records the corrupt input had not touched as misidentified, built from garbage. The same comparison sorted the corrupt record itself into "read as unedited" and "built from garbage", so a parser that validates no checksum and reads imprecisely would have had input 1 failed as garbage where owner decision 2 says to report it (shown with a probe, below). Why it looked right: the three built-in adapters read these records exactly or within the tolerance, so on every run before the first library the two questions gave the same answer. PyEphem 4.2.1 keeps seven fields in single precision and no second derivative (round one's findings, which every values item already reports); it reads both neighbours in every file exactly as it reads them with no corrupt input between them [tested], and the item failed four times. The same class as the reverse collapse D-144 found, where per-file checks passed on an empty output once an adapter could refuse the only record in a file: a check whose verdict rested on something other than its question, and which looked right until something ran against it. There a pass was earned by absence; here a fail was earned by a different question. The change: `tools/derive_corrupt_inputs.py` writes three unedited files beside the inputs, `derived/corrupt-input/unedited-sets.tle` for inputs 1 to 4, `unedited-rows.csv` with input 5's last row whole and `unedited-array.json` closed, provenance `derived`, each input being its unedited file with its one edit made (a test checks it byte for byte); the six inputs are unchanged, byte for byte. `expected.json` lists the three as sources, nine in all, names each input's unedited file, and keeps the records' expected values for reference, saying that this case no longer grades against them. The runner reads each unedited file once and compares every record returned from an input with that reading, exactly, so any difference is the edit's doing. A record the parser does not return from the unedited file either is not graded; an input whose unedited file the parser refuses as a whole is not exercised; and a record beyond the expected ones counts as built from garbage only when the unedited reading lacks it too; so nothing a parser does to these records anyway is blamed on the edit. How exactly a parser reads them stays the values check's question, asked in the records' own cases, `epoch-year-19xx` and `bstar-and-derivative-forms`. The case is unreleased, so no released check semantics change; v0.4.0 ships it with the comparison. What moved [tested, item by item against the earlier runs]: every status of the three built-in adapters stays, except that python-sgp4's four neighbours items move from pass within tolerance to exact pass, since the comparison is now with its own reading; texts that named expected values now name the unedited reading; `docs/FAILURES.md` changes in three such texts and its timestamp, and its failing counts (naive 15 of 18, python-sgp4 8 of 18) do not move. PyEphem 4.2.1's row re-stated: 7 of 18, as under D-174; the four neighbours items move from fail to pass, no other item changes status, and the gate is identical; the detail is in the handoff note. An adapter with PyEphem's precision over the reference readers, so that it handles every corrupt input exactly as the reference does, failed five items under the old comparison, input 1 among them, and passes under the new one with the reference's items [tested: a probe kept in `docs/handoff/corrupt-input/`]. Also changed: the case's check texts, evidence basis and coverage, its interpretation in the manifest, the README's case row and the changelog's entry say what is compared; `manifest.json`, `MANIFEST.md` and the case's `case.md` regenerated; `tests/test_corrupt_input.py` gains six tests: the unedited files against the inputs, the precision adapter passing where the values check fails it, a neighbour the corrupt input changes failing, a parser that cannot read the unedited file not exercised, a record it does not return unedited not graded, and a record it returns unedited too not blamed on the edit. Suites: 287 tests OK with PyEphem installed (6 skipped), with python-sgp4 installed (6 skipped) and with neither (9 skipped); CI's two offline commands exit 0 and 1 as before; an export would copy 228 files, six more than before [tested, exit codes checked]. Alternatives: comparing at each field's printed resolution (rejected: PyEphem's missing second derivative still fails it, and a parser that keeps one field with fewer digits than the TLE prints would fail on precision again); keeping the expected values and exempting the fields a parser fails in the values check (rejected: it ties this case to other cases' results and to provider data a pip-installed runner may not have); undoing each edit at run time instead of shipping the unedited files (rejected: the runner would need each edit's inverse, where a file on disk can be read and pinned by its hash); falling back to the expected values where a parser does not return a record unedited (rejected: it brings the conflation back for exactly the parsers it would serve).

**D-176 (2026-09-27) — v0.4.0 stage 3, library 2 of 8: satellite.js 7.1.0 re-run against the eighteen cases through the `satellite.js` preset as shipped (owner instruction), and compared item by item with round one's run (D-132), which was reproduced first; findings recorded and kept privately at `docs/handoff/batch-g/satellitejs/round-2/`, outside the export allowlist; nothing filed.** Versions, by the owner's rule for this round: satellite.js's latest release is still 7.1.0 of 2026-07-23, the version of round one; the fix for round one's epoch finding (#186) is merged on the library's development branch and not released [tested: npm and GitHub, 2026-09-27]. Nothing was downloaded: the scratchpad's Node project holds 7.1.0 from the npm registry, unpatched [tested: its lockfile and the release's source line that round one's patch replaces]. The baseline: round one's run was reproduced from a detached worktree at private 1cc7f40, whose runner, cases and fixtures are those of fff9b6b, the commit that made round one's working tree (D-129 and D-131), with the provider files git does not track present, and with round one's harness; its case table and every fail and pass-tolerance line are identical to the recorded run [tested]. Round one's note gives that runner as 5d49d99 with D-129 uncommitted, but the run's texts carry D-131's wording, which the reproduction confirms; the note is left as written and this entry records the correction. No harness change: the preset is round one's harness with its parsing unchanged, and the refusal channel is not adopted for this library (the owner's list, and section 8 of the v0.4.0 plan, give it to the four libraries that raise on a malformed line); it would have had nothing to carry, since neither of the library's readers raised in any case of either run [tested]. Result: 8 of 18 cases fail (round one: 7 of 17). Against round one, item by item: all 222 of its items are unchanged in status and text; six empty-answer items are new passes (D-143), which move one case from skip to pass; the new case adds eleven items and is the eighth failing case; nothing else moved to or from fail. Of the new case's three failing items, one rests on the harness's line pairing, which never hands the library a line 1 with no line 2 after it; the pass on the JSON array left open is the harness's too, since the preset parses the text itself and the library takes one parsed record at a time. Findings about satellite.js were recorded (D-134). Observed on the way, about the corpus: a command adapter's standard error reaches an item's detail with its trailing newline, since the command parser does not strip it; cosmetic, left for a decision rather than changed during a library run. Alternatives: installing the library afresh (not needed: the existing install was checked against the registry and the release's source); adopting the refusal channel anyway (rejected: not the owner's instruction, and it would report nothing). **The letter finding filed 2026-09-28T00:30:19Z by the owner as https://github.com/shashwatak/satellite-js/issues/190**, with the reference `docs/LETTER-IN-FIELD.md` (D-193); the other stays private until it is reported (link added under the D-134 convention).

**D-177 (2026-09-27) — A failing command adapter's standard error reaches an item's detail without the line ending it came with (owner instruction, on the observation recorded in D-176).** The command parser built the error for a non-zero exit, under `--cmd` and under `--write-cmd`, from the last 300 characters of the command's standard error as they came, so the line ending most programs end with went into the item's detail and broke the report's line: the satellite.js preset on the JSON array left open gave "... (line 1 column 1279)\n): fail-closed ...". The text is now stripped of the whitespace around it before the last 300 characters are taken, in one helper that both places use. What it touches: only the text of an item that carries a failing command's error. No status can move, since the error is raised in the same conditions as before and nothing reads its text. Checked [tested]: the satellite.js preset over all eighteen cases, compared item by item with its D-176 run: 238 of 239 items identical, one text changed, that item's, no status moved, the gate identical. `docs/FAILURES.md` is built from the three built-in adapters, which are Python and never pass through the command parser, and carries no such text, so it was not regenerated; no tracked file outside the private handoff carries one. The D-176 run's files stay as that run's record, with the re-run beside them. Line endings inside the text, from a command that writes several lines before it fails, stay as written: not asked, and no run so far has produced one [tested: the one such text in the handoff is satellite.js's]. Tests: `tests/test_negative.py` compares the failing command's error whole ("exit 1: boom: cannot read"), checks that the runner's parse item carries no line ending, and names a failing writer command without its line endings; all three fail against the parser as it was. Suites: 288 tests OK in the virtual environment with python-sgp4 (6 skipped) and under the system Python 3.14 (9 skipped); CI's two offline commands exit 0 and 1 as before. **D-183 (2026-09-27) — Every harness hands each line 1 to the library with whatever line follows it, so that the corrupt-input case's input 4 measures the library's answer to a set missing its second line, not the harness's pairing (owner decision); the six libraries of round two done so far re-run under it. The same class of error as D-175.** What the input was measuring: input 4 gives a set whose line 1 is followed by the next set's name line. Every adapter over a library that reads one set at a time (the `pyephem`, `sgp4`, `satellite.js` and `tle.js` presets and the Gpredict and libsgp4 recipes) split the file itself and handed the library a pair only when a line 2 followed a line 1, so the orphan line 1 never reached the library, and the item graded the harness's pairing: it failed as "dropped" for every such library, whatever the library would have done with the set. As with D-175, the check measured our code, not theirs: there the neighbours item graded a parser's precision instead of what the corrupt input changed, here input 4 graded the harness's line pairing instead of the library; both looked right while the built-in adapters' answers matched the check's assumption, and both surfaced when a real library ran against them, this one with libsgp4 (D-182), whose one failing item on the case was the harness's. The change: each of the six hands every line 1 to the library with the line after it, a line 2 or not (an empty line when the file ends there), and the library's answer is what the report shows: a record, an error, or a refusal through the channel where the harness has one. Nothing else moves: a valid file has a line 2 after every line 1. The applications that read the file themselves (gods-eye-view, SatDump) and the library whose own iterator walks it (astroz) had nothing to change; the corpus's reference and naive adapters are its own readers, not harnesses over a library, and keep their reading. `docs/ADAPTERS.md` asks the same of a new adapter. Re-runs, item by item against each library's latest round-two run [tested]: PyEphem 4.2.1, input 4 from fail to pass (the library refuses the pair: "line does not conform to tle format"), row unchanged at 7 of 18; satellite.js 7.1.0, a finding about satellite.js recorded (D-134), row unchanged at 8 of 18; Gpredict 2.6, input 4 from fail to pass (the function returns -2), row unchanged at 7 of 18; gods-eye-view and SatDump, every item identical (179, 215 and 167), rows unchanged; libsgp4 v3.0, input 4 from fail to pass ("Invalid length for line two"), so the corrupt-input case passes for it and its row moves from 10 of 18 to 9 of 18. The built-in python-sgp4 adapter: input 4 still fails, now on the library's own answer, since its accelerated class returns a satellite of zeros with `error` 2 for the pair where its pure-Python class raises, the split python-sgp4 #116 documents; python-sgp4 still fails 8 of 18, `docs/FAILURES.md` changes in that line and its timestamp, and the reference and naive reports are unchanged item for item. Recipes: `harnesses/gpredict/harness.c` and `harnesses/libsgp4/harness.cpp` changed, with their copies in the handoff notes that the recipe test compares them to, and their READMEs say so and give the counts. Tests: `tests/test_presets.py` now requires the PyEphem preset to hand the orphan line 1 to `readtle()` and report exactly what it raises, and the python-sgp4 adapter to pass the library's answer on; 289 tests OK in the virtual environment with python-sgp4 (6 skipped), with PyEphem (7 skipped) and under the system Python 3.14 (10 skipped) [tested, exit codes checked]. Alternatives: letting an adapter declare that it pairs lines itself, with input 4 then not exercised for it (rejected by the owner: it leaves the library's answer unmeasured); keeping the pairing and crediting the item to the harness in the notes alone (rejected: the report, which is what a reader sees, would still grade our code).

**D-186 (2026-09-27) — Convention for outreach text, recorded in `CLAUDE.md` (owner instruction): a message to another project leads with the one-sentence finding, then one reproduction and one measured number, and stops; no [tested]/[inferred] brackets in filed text; no tables, section headers or bulleted inventories in a first report; file:line citations only where the line is the point; the evidence lives in the corpus and the message points at it.** Reason: a SatDump collaborator asked on #1221 not to be sent LLM-generated text, which is hard to read, and to focus on the actual issue (https://github.com/SatDump/SatDump/issues/1221#issuecomment-5857824841); the owner adopted that for all outreach. The verification standard behind every claim is unchanged: the markers and the evidence stay in the handoff and the corpus, and only how much of it goes in the message changes. Reference lengths, in words of the posted text, measured through GitHub's API on 2026-09-27: the original #1221 report, 459 words, the length that drew the complaint; the #44 comment on dnwrnr/sgp4 after v3.0 (194 words), the astroz #99 confirmation (128) and the reply on #1221 (39), the length that has worked. The first two predate the convention and carry a [tested] bracket each, so they are cited for their length only. A draft written before the convention is checked against it before it goes out; the unfiled ones were re-read on 2026-09-27, and the result is in the private handoff. The re-read found one stale status line: `docs/upstream/python-sgp4-xml-empty-object-id.md` still said "not yet filed", although the draft was filed as python-sgp4 #171 (D-086) and #171 closed when pull request #172 merged (D-130); it is corrected, on the owner's instruction. The two drafts the re-read found in need of rewriting, the tle.js pull request and the python-sgp4 classification draft, stay as they are until they are actually sent (owner decision). `CLAUDE.md` is on the export allowlist, so the convention and its citations publish with the next export. Alternatives: the handoff alone (not taken: the owner asked for `CLAUDE.md`, which every session reads, so that the convention applies to every future session).

**D-188 (2026-09-27) — "The catalog passed 99,999" is accurate and stands (owner decision, after a review claimed that public copy drifts toward it); the one wrong sentence was a different claim, about field capacity, and it appeared in exactly one place, the profile README, which is corrected.** The review's claim: public-facing copy says "the catalog passed 99,999" where the primary CelesTrak fact is the five-digit ceiling at 69999 and the Alpha-5 transition. The owner's finding: the catalog did pass 99,999 when number 100000 was assigned to SARAMAGO on 2026-07-11, so the phrase is true; the review conflated it with a different sentence, the claim that 99,999 is the most the five-digit field or range holds, which is wrong: CelesTrak's FAQ puts the end of the five-digit catalog numbers at 69999 (`docs/RESEARCH.md`, section 1). Searched on 2026-09-27 [tested]: this repository and its public export, the site's repository, the live profile README, the GitHub descriptions of the three repositories, the account's bio and the corpus's four release notes, for every variant of the event wording (passed, crossed, exceeded, went past, reached 100000, outgrew, ran out of five-digit) and, separately, for any claim tying the five-digit field or range to 99,999. The capacity claim appeared once: the profile README's "passed 99,999 objects, the most a TLE's five-digit field can hold", rewritten to the corpus README's sentence (number 100000 assigned to SARAMAGO after the five-digit range, which ends at 69999, was exhausted) in hneogy/hneogy commit 7d90517, pushed on the owner's word at 2026-09-27T18:20:39Z (5488b43..7d90517); the live README, read back through GitHub's API, carries the new sentence and not the old one [tested]. Everything else stands, and is not to be re-opened: the corpus repository's GitHub description ("after the satellite catalog passed 99,999"); the site's front page (the title "Satellite numbers outgrew their format", "The world ran out of five-digit satellite numbers", "reached number 100000", the last two CelesTrak's own facts); the Libre Space forum thread's title; the same phrase in gods-eye-view #751, in PR #767's code comment and in SatDump #1221; and every "above 99,999", which describes the numbers that need Alpha-5 or six digits, not an event. Also correct, and not the capacity claim: "99999, the largest five-digit number" (the writer case's vector id) and the site's TLE tool, "plain digits up to 99999", which states what the field can encode, not where the catalog's five-digit range ended. Alternatives: rewording every "passed 99,999" (not taken, by the owner: the phrase is accurate).

**D-189 (2026-09-27) — Three decisions for the v0.4.0 release (owner): D-183 reworded to the D-134 form before the export; the site's second-round Batch G rows wait for the second-round reports, libsgp4 and astroz apart; no reproducible package builds in 0.4.0.** (1) D-183's line on satellite.js stated the specifics of a finding not yet reported to the project, which the export would have published; it now names the library and says that a finding was recorded, as D-134 asks, and the specifics stay in the private handoff. The entry was reworded in place because it had not been exported; the earlier wording is in the private repository's history. The finding is not reported in order to unblock the release (owner). A scan of D-171 to D-188 for the round-two findings, read against the private notes on 2026-09-27, found no other entry carrying an unreported finding's specifics. (2) The site's hand-run table: the libsgp4 and astroz rows can take their second-round results, since neither library has an unreported finding from the round; the rows of PyEphem, satellite.js, Gpredict, gods-eye-view, SatDump and tle.js stay at round one until each library's second-round report is filed. **D-193 (2026-09-28) — The letter-in-field reference, `docs/LETTER-IN-FIELD.md`, published after the four reports it goes with (owner decisions): what the TLE readers it names do with the corrupt-input case's input 3, the letter O in place of a 0 in line 1's epoch field; reported to PyEphem (#297), satellite.js (#190), Gpredict (#427) and tle.js (#63).** The finding: four readers accept the line and read the field up to the letter, the epoch 14 min 16.17 s early, with no error; the TLE checksum cannot catch it, since a letter counts 0 as the 0 did; two readers refuse it, libsgp4 v3.0 by checking the field's characters before converting, astroz v0.14.0 through Zig's number reader, which takes the whole string or fails. The shape (owner): one shared reference in the corpus, public and versioned and hosted by no maintainer, and one short note to each maintainer under the outreach convention (D-186), each a new issue with the one-sentence finding, one reproduction and one measured number. The page carries the evidence, what was run kept apart from what was read, every source link at the version run [tested: each cited line fetched at its tag, every link answering, 2026-09-27]; it covers only the readers named on it and says it is not a survey. libsgp4 and astroz are cited in the page, for their refusal and how they refuse, and not in the notes. Each note marks that column 26 of line 1 is the letter O, as a comment on the line or, in the Gpredict note, whose block is TLE text, as a sentence under it, since the letter is indistinguishable from a zero in most monospace fonts (owner). Order (owner): the notes filed first, without the link, one at a time with a preview and the owner's go for each; then the page pushed; then each note edited to add one line linking the page, since a dead link in four inboxes is worse than four one-line edits. Filed [tested: GitHub's API, each title and body identical to the approved text]: https://github.com/brandon-rhodes/pyephem/issues/297 at 00:07:08Z from the owner's account on the owner's go; https://github.com/shashwatak/satellite-js/issues/190 at 00:30:19Z, https://github.com/csete/gpredict/issues/427 at 00:43:21Z and https://github.com/davidcalhoun/tle.js/issues/63 at 00:46:33Z, each created by the owner from the form staged in the app's browser. Every code block in the notes was re-run as written before filing (PyEphem 4.2.1, satellite.js 7.1.0 and tle.js 5.0.3; Gpredict's set through the recipe's harness at v2.6). D-174, D-176, D-178 and D-184 take their links under the D-134 convention. Alternatives: one issue per library carrying the evidence (not taken: D-186); the page first and the notes after (not taken, by the owner: never before); the link in each note at filing (not taken, by the owner: it would be dead until the push).

**D-194 (2026-09-28) — v0.5.0 planned, not built (owner decisions): one release, holding reproducible package builds, an "unsupported" answer for vector hooks run by command, and the gate's counts, in that order; and a finding settled: the gate's outcomes had two definitions, shipped together in v0.3.0 and v0.4.0, and v0.5.0 makes the one that splits the records authoritative.** The finding: the README's gate section ("records are counted as loaded ..., misidentified ... or dropped (no record came back)") and `gpconf/gates.py`'s docstring (D-142: "Per record and format there are four outcomes ... dropped (no record and no refusal)") define one outcome per expected record, and every gate line published on the corpus's site follows them; `docs/ADAPTERS.md` (D-161: "A record returned with the wrong catalog number is counted twice, as a misidentified record and as a dropped expected one: an adapter that returns 0 for every Alpha-5 field of a 256-record file shows 256 misidentified and 256 dropped") and the runner's comment over the counts define `dropped` per expected id, so a record returned under a wrong id counts twice, and the runner's arithmetic and headline follow that [tested: the texts at both tags]. On the v0.4.0 hand runs that doubles 256 records for four libraries and 1 for a fifth [tested: each run's `gates`]. The split is authoritative, not to be reopened: it answers the gate's own question, one outcome per object, where the double count sums to more objects than there are; it is what three of the four published descriptions and every published gate line already say, so nothing published moves; the adapter guide's sentence described the code after it was written, while gates.py's docstring is the definition the gate was designed to; and nothing is lost, the per-id count staying in the report under its own name. v0.5.0 therefore gives `dropped` the split meaning (the expected records with no record returned at all and no refusal), keeps today's count as `ids_not_returned`, prints the headline from the split, and withdraws the adapter guide's sentence quoted above; changing `dropped`'s value for parsers that misidentify stays within a minor release, since the gate is a headline and never a check and no `expected.json` schema changes. The other two items: `tools/stage_package.py` takes `SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH` (the tag's commit time), stamps the staged files with it and fixed modes, and passes it to the build, normalising the sdist if two builds still differ, verified by two builds giving identical SHA-256s [untested until built]; a vectors command may answer `{"unsupported": "<reason>"}`, which skips that hook's item as a missing Python hook does, an additive protocol change (a minor version under the README's rule), chosen over exit 3 because a non-zero exit already means a rejection there. The vector change moves only items of runs that pass a vectors command, those of three hand-run libraries, and no case count [tested: the round-two reports]. One release, because the vector change needs a minor version anyway, the gate fix fits it, the build change takes effect only when a release is built, and none of the three moves a published case count or site figure. The plan is in the private handoff (`docs/handoff/v0.5.0-plan.md`). Alternatives: the double count authoritative (not taken: it would move the README, gates.py, the site's definitions and a published line, and it cannot be read as an answer to the gate's question); keeping `dropped` as the adapter guide documents it and adding a split count beside it (not taken: two meanings of one word, one in the headline and one in the JSON); three releases (not taken: three checklists for no change a reader would see between them).

**D-195 (2026-09-28) — satellite.js: a reply posted on issue #190 from the owner's account, on the owner's word, https://github.com/shashwatak/satellite-js/issues/190#issuecomment-5870894160 (2026-09-28T13:32:08Z); and pull request #187 merged the same day as 984c3d1, awaiting a release.** 1valdis, a collaborator, asked on #190 at 08:22:06Z (https://github.com/shashwatak/satellite-js/issues/190#issuecomment-5866216889) whether swapping `parseInt()` and `parseFloat()` for `Number()` where possible, with a check for NaN, would do. The reply, four sentences under the outreach convention (D-186): yes, `Number()` with a NaN check is the right shape, since it rejects a field that is not wholly numeric where the two parse functions return the longest numeric prefix; one trap, the one the owner hit in `alpha5ToNumber` and fixed in ef05a72 (#187): `Number('')` and `Number('   ')` return 0, not NaN, and TLE fields are space-padded, so a field of only spaces would come back as zero; trimming and rejecting an empty string first, or a regex, handles it; and an offer to run the corpus against a branch. Checked before posting [tested: Node 26.5.0]: `Number` gives NaN for `26189.7O990935` and for `12A45`, where `parseFloat` gives 26189.7 and 12 and `parseInt` 26189 and 12; `Number` gives 0 for an empty field and for one of spaces, where `parseFloat` and `parseInt` give NaN, so the swap alone would turn a blank field from NaN into 0; ef05a72 is #187's commit "give NaN for a blank catalog number field in alpha5ToNumber". The body GitHub stored is identical to the approved text, and GitHub linked the hash to the commit and #187 to the pull request [tested: GitHub's API]. Pull request #187 (`alpha5ToNumber`, which decodes an Alpha-5 catalog number field) was merged into develop by 1valdis at 08:44:25Z, squashed as 984c3d1, so ef05a72 survives as a commit of the pull request; no release carries it, npm's latest being 7.1.0 of 2026-07-23 [tested: GitHub's API and the npm registry, 2026-09-28]. The site's label moved under S-069 (aec2352, pushed on the owner's word): "merged 2026-09-28; Alpha-5 decoder in develop, awaiting a release"; the row stays as run against 7.1.0 until a run of a release is published. #190 stays open.

**D-196 (2026-10-01) — The README's Alpha-5 sentence corrected (owner instruction): the first two digits of a six-digit number are written as one letter and the last four are kept; it said "the first digit is replaced by a letter".** The old sentence describes a one-for-one swap, which cannot turn six digits into five characters and contradicts the corpus's own rule and vectors: `vectors/alpha5.json` states "first character is a letter with value 10-33 (A=10, B=11, ... skipping I and O ... Z=33) followed by the last four digits", and 100000 is `A0000`, 10 and 0000 [tested: the file's `rule` and its official examples]. Found when the site's story page was given an explorer for the encoding (site S-071), whose own four sentences were corrected the same way; the README is what a developer reads before the site, so the two now state the project's core concept alike. No vector, expected value, check or code changes. Searched for the old phrasing: none left in `README.md`, `docs/`, `gpconf/` or `tools/` [tested: grep]. It goes public with the next export, not one of its own (owner); the PyPI page of 0.4.0 carries the old sentence until the next release replaces it, a release's description being fixed once uploaded [inferred: PyPI serves the description uploaded with each release].

**D-197 (2026-10-01) — Two replies posted from the owner's account, on the owner's word, each body read back identical through GitHub's API: on satellite.js #190, the scoping question for the `Number()` pull request (06:24:57Z, https://github.com/shashwatak/satellite-js/issues/190#issuecomment-5925951118); on PyEphem #297, the answer to the maintainer's three points, the case's origin first (06:47:42Z, https://github.com/brandon-rhodes/pyephem/issues/297#issuecomment-5926220791). The #297 reply promises a README line naming Vallado's C++ as part of the stack, through python-sgp4's accelerated class; it rides with the next export.** #190: 1valdis asked on 2026-09-29T18:56:42Z (issuecomment-5896650368) whether to do the swap themself or take a pull request from the owner. The reply, three sentences under the outreach convention (D-186): yes, the owner opens the PR; one scoping question, the swap on all eleven conversions in `twoline2satrec` or only on the epoch field from the report, since the full swap changes how every numeric field is read and that is the collaborator's call. Checked before drafting [tested: develop 984c3d1, every file under `src/` fetched]: every `parseInt`/`parseFloat` under `src/` is in `twoline2satrec`, `src/io.ts` lines 88-107, eleven calls (epoch year, epoch day, the two derivatives, B*, and the six elements of line 2); the catalog number is not converted there (line 86 keeps columns 3-7 as a string, `json2satrec` line 197 takes `NORAD_CAT_ID.toString()`), so the first wording of the question, "just the catalog-number path", named a path that does not exist and would have left the report's own reproduction, the epoch day, uncovered; the narrow option offered is the field of the report. Kept out of the reply, for the PR: `error` is a constant 0 in both readers and nothing in `io.ts` checks for NaN, so how a refused field is signalled is undecided upstream. The PR waits for the scope answer and the owner's go. #297: brandon-rhodes replied on 2026-09-30T19:20:37Z (issuecomment-5918070515) that PyEphem's SGP4 handling follows Vallado's C++ reference (celestrak.org/publications/AIAA/2006-6753/), which he took to accept and truncate the field the same way, so that PyEphem would be correct by the standard it implements; that the README names no Vallado implementation, which would be a useful addition; and asking how the case arose, since he had never met it in practice, unless the archives showed O-for-0 to be real. The answer is in the owner's words, reordered by the owner to put the origin first: the case was made, not found, no archive searched, no claim that anyone has hit it in the wild; the corrupt-input case's shape and its five edits, the O chosen because it is the one edit the checksum cannot catch; the same habit as #296, a prefix conversion with no check that the field was consumed; no standard claimed, the check's expectation the corpus's own and what PyEphem does the maintainer's decision; then the Vallado finding; then the README line. The origin as stated matches the record (v0.4.0 plan note sections 3 and 5, D-171: a designed fail-closed case) and the repository holds no archive search [tested: grep]. The Vallado finding [tested: `docs/handoff/batch-g/vallado/`, a harness of this corpus against python-sgp4's unmodified `extension/SGP4.cpp`, Vallado's revision of 2023-05-09 per python-sgp4's fac882a, its line-1 `sscanf` changed only by Vallado's own `%5ld` to `%5s`]: `twoline2rv` reads the epoch field to the letter, day 189.7, and the `sscanf` over line 1 stops there, so ndot, nddot, B* and the element-set number are never read (sentinels kept), `error` stays 0, line 2 is read in full; python-sgp4 2.27's `Satrec.twoline2rv` returns them as 0, B* 0 for -0.70517e-5, and its pure-Python reader raises `ValueError` [tested: the corpus venv, accelerated]; so the reference gives up the rest of line 1 without a word, and PyEphem, which reads every later field right (D-193's run), is ahead of it on this line, which the reply says. Untested, and said so in the reply: the celestrak.org archive itself (no CelesTrak request under the standing rule) and whether python-sgp4's copy is byte-identical to it. One sentence of the reply is looser than the record: "Same code path" for #296 and #297, which are two call sites of the same habit, `(long) strtod(l1+2, 0)` in `extensions/_libastro.c` for `catalog_number` and `tle_fld()` to `atod()` to `ascii_strtod()` in `libastro/dbfmt.c` and `misc.c` for the epoch; flagged before posting, kept by the owner. Format: the owner's hard wraps unwrapped into paragraphs, with six single line breaks kept where a line is one statement (the five edits, the O-for-0 choice, each measured observation), since GitHub renders a newline inside a comment as a line break (D-133); the preview checked before submitting, 9 paragraphs, 6 breaks, no emphasis from the `B*` asterisks, #296 linked twice [tested]. The README line is a commitment: met in the next export beside D-196 (handoff item). Not promised: Vallado's C++ as a hand-run library of its own, which the maintainer invited; a proposal for v0.5.0, the owner's call.

**D-198 (2026-10-01) — PyEphem #297 closed as completed by its maintainer at 2026-10-01T12:14:28Z, with no change to `readtle()`, open to comment and to reopening; his suggestion, to call Vallado's C++ directly rather than through python-sgp4, which carries small backwards-compatibility tweaks of its own, becomes the specification of the Vallado harness in the v0.5.0 plan (owner); the README line of D-197 stands either way (owner).** The closing comment (https://github.com/brandon-rhodes/pyephem/issues/297#issuecomment-5931180707; the closure at the same second, `state_reason` completed, the issue not locked [tested: GitHub's API]) says, in order: thanks for the context; that we should see if we can call Vallado's C++ directly, perhaps through a simple C compatibility tool that reads and writes the struct, because python-sgp4 makes a few small tweaks of its own that were necessary for backwards compatibility for its user base, so that calling Vallado ourselves would be an independent data point on how the reference library behaves; that since neither side has seen the letter in the wild, and PyEphem is a legacy library he is trying not to add code to, he closes the issue for now; and that further thoughts are welcome as comments, with reopening later if a future situation warrants adding input validation where, historically, there has been none. What this changes in the record: the D-197 Vallado finding was made on python-sgp4's copy of `SGP4.cpp`, which the maintainer now says is not Vallado's code untouched. python-sgp4's own history shows one change to the line-1 `sscanf` of `twoline2rv`, Vallado's `%5ld` to `%5s` for Alpha-5, and nothing else in that function's parsing [tested: `git log -p` on the file], so the tweaks he means are elsewhere in the file or in the initial copy, unknown until the archive is fetched; the handoff's open question on byte-identity is answered in part (not identical) and narrowed to which lines. The finding as stated, the `sscanf` stopping at the letter with the rest of line 1 unread, is not withdrawn: it is a result on python-sgp4's copy, and whether a tweak elsewhere changes what the unread fields hold is the archive's question. The v0.5.0 plan takes the suggestion as the harness's specification: fetch the archive linked from celestrak.org/publications/AIAA/2006-6753/ (one CelesTrak request, on the owner's word), compile its `twoline2rv` behind a harness of the corpus's own that fills and reads the `elsetrec` directly, as `batch-g/vallado/harness.cpp` already does against python-sgp4's copy, and run the cases through a recipe in `harnesses/`, as libsgp4's; the data point is independent of python-sgp4 only when the archive's code is what runs. The site's label for #297 moves under S-072, the row's finding and count unchanged, since a closure changes no measurement. The README line of D-197 is still owed: a closed issue does not change what the stack contains, and the promise was made in the thread.

**D-199 (2026-10-01) — The satellite.js preset emits a NaN epoch as null and notes it on stderr, as it already did for the other non-finite fields; a harness fix, found by running the branch for #190's pull request: before, `isoEpoch()` built the string `2026-NaNTNaN:NaN:NaN.000NaN` from a NaN day and the runner reported "internal error in runner", its own fault, in place of the library's answer. The pull request branch is built and kept locally with its evidence, not pushed (owner review first).** The branch (owner instruction: all eleven conversions, one style): a clone of upstream develop 984c3d1 in the session scratchpad, branch `fix/twoline2satrec-number-conversions`, one commit 6779411 that moves the eleven `parseInt`/`parseFloat` calls of `twoline2satrec` (`src/io.ts` 88-107) to `Number()` and adds three tests on the #190 line (NaN epoch, the neighbouring fields read, the unedited line as before); saved as `docs/handoff/batch-g/satellitejs/pr-number-conversions/0001-*.patch` with the description draft `PR.md`, both corpus reports, the run logs, `compare.txt` and the two scans with their outputs. Their suite on the branch [tested]: the vitest js project, 422 tests, the type tests and Biome (exit 0; three pre-existing warnings in `test/wasm/bulk-propagator.test-d.ts` and `biome.jsonc`, untouched) pass; the wasm projects need an emscripten build this machine lacks and were not run. The regression scan (owner instruction, before the branch) [tested: `scan.js`, `fields.js`, Node 26.5.0]: every TLE line in the private corpus, 1,826 distinct lines (913 of each kind, 10,043 field strings), handed to the eleven conversions as `io.ts` builds the strings, gives the same value from `parseFloat` and `Number` except the letter line's epoch (189.7 against NaN); the 908 records with frozen TLE fields in the eighteen `expected.json` files (5,459 strings) and the epoch vectors, 0 differences; no exponent in the corpus has a space for its sign (second derivative + 815 and - 93, B* - 897 and + 11). The corpus run through the preset with `--module` against the develop build and the branch build [tested: reports kept]: 18 cases, 239 items each, 237 identical; the two that differ are corrupt-input items that fail either way: the letter line's epoch, 189.7 before and null after (the library returns a record with NaN and `error` 0, not a refusal, so the corpus still counts a record built from the corrupt input), and the orphan line 1 of input 4, whose mean motion goes from null to 0 because the name line's columns 53-63 are an empty substring and `Number('')` is 0, the trap of D-195's reply shown by the corpus itself; the case statuses do not move. With the preset as shipped the branch run crashed the corrupt-input case on the NaN epoch, which is this entry's fix: `record()` emits `epoch: null` when the year or day is not finite and writes `non-finite epoch for <id>` to stderr; the develop build gives the identical 239 items with the fixed preset [tested: compared item by item]. Test: `tests/test_node_presets.py` `SatelliteJsNaNEpoch`, a stub module returning the letter line's record with a NaN day through the real harness [tested: passes; the test fails on the harness before the fix, exit 1, AssertionError on the epoch]. The adapter guide's excerpt of the preset (`docs/ADAPTERS.md`, D-161) regenerated, lines 8 to 82 now, which its test caught [tested: suite 290 OK, 6 skipped]. Open for the owner: the push and the pull request; whether a one-line helper with a trim-and-empty check over `Number()`, used for all eleven, goes in (it keeps a blank field at NaN, the owner's own advice on #190; not in the branch, which is the plain swap 1valdis asked for); how a NaN field is signalled, proposed in the description, not settled. Noted, not acted on: the develop build fails 2 of 18 against the corpus where 7.1.0 failed 8 of 18, #186 and #187 being in develop; the site's row moves only when a release ships (S-049).

"Allow edits by maintainers" left on. The description leads with the regression evidence of D-199, names the two observed differences in one sentence (the letter line, the fix; the orphan line 1, a regression on malformed input), keeps the forms the corpus does not hold to their own paragraph, points at their suite as the other half, and proposes the NaN signalling (a `SatRecError` member, or throwing, or NaN documented) without settling it; the trim-and-empty helper offered, not included (owner). The site: the row's reports take the pull request's label under S-073, "PR #197 (open 2026-10-02; the eleven field conversions moved to Number, from #190)", the row itself staying at round one (S-056: other round-two findings unreported; #190 is filed, so naming it discloses nothing). Open: review by 1valdis; the signalling decision; a release that carries #186, #187 and this.

**D-201 (2026-10-02) — python-sgp4 #169 moved on 2026-10-01: its maintainer found that CelesTrak's GitHub repository holds the current Vallado C++, with `satnum` an integer again beside a new `satnumStr[6]`, removed "needs more information", committed to copying that code in and pivoting his Python, and credited the owner's comment with bringing him back to the thread. Recorded with what it changes here: CelesTrak's GitHub, not the AIAA-2006-6753 archive, is the live source of the reference; the v0.5.0 Vallado harness takes it at a pinned commit, outside D-023; and python-sgp4's copy of the two files is byte-identical to that repository's initial import, so the "tweaks" of D-198 are not in them.** The comment (https://github.com/brandon-rhodes/python-sgp4/issues/169#issuecomment-5931853562, 12:51:31Z; the label removed at 12:51:01Z [tested: GitHub's API]): michaelbaisch's link of 2026-08-29 to `software/cpp/SGP4/SGP4/SGP4.h` in CelesTrak/fundamentals-of-astrodynamics; he had checked the AIAA-2006-6753 zip for updates for years and found none, while the repository shows recent activity; `satnum` flipped back to an integer with the six-character string as `satnumStr`; he must copy the new C++ in and pivot the satnum handling to keep packaging the latest Vallado code, which gives him somewhere to store big integers. The repository [tested: GitHub's API and raw files, 2026-10-02]: created 2024-03-29; licence AGPL-3.0 for the repository, with the SGP4 C++ folder carved out by its README and NOTICE as derived from AIAA 2006-6753 and retaining "its original unrestricted terms"; `SGP4.cpp` last changed by 7e0078a (2026-08-31, "fix integer to string casting (#169)") after e959a99 (2026-08-30, "Invalid sscanf argument (#165)") and 190b77f (2025-12-30, "accept alpha5 and convert to int"), the header now "30 dec 25 add alpha5 support"; `SGP4.h` by 190b77f after be2c563 (2025-12-09). The byte test: python-sgp4's `extension/SGP4.cpp` and `SGP4.h` are identical, byte for byte with their CRLF endings, to the repository's initial import 80c8821 of 2024-03-29 (110,786 bytes, SHA-256 2ee7ad0e for the .cpp) [tested: raw files compared]; so the handoff's open question on the archive is answered as far as evidence reaches without a celestrak.org request: the copy we ran is Vallado's code as CelesTrak first imported it, and the maintainer's "few small tweaks" are not in these two files; where they are, if anywhere, is his to say. That the archive is stale is his statement and CelesTrak's NOTICE, not our test [untested]. Against the current main: the line-1 `sscanf` format is unchanged, and the harness of D-197, pointed at the main copy, reads the letter line the same way [tested: `batch-g/vallado/github-run.txt`]; the main copy decodes an Alpha-5 field into the integer `satnum`, exercised there on the corpus's vectors, with findings about the reference's decoder recorded privately in `batch-g/vallado/` under the Batch G convention; reported on 2026-10-02 as https://github.com/CelesTrak/fundamentals-of-astrodynamics/issues/172 (D-204). D-023 governs raw provider bytes and the fetch script under CelesTrak's data terms; a git source at github.com is neither, and the standing no-request rule names celestrak.org, so the harness is governed by D-155's recipe conventions (a pinned commit, build commands, nothing of the library's copied in beyond what its terms allow) and by the NOTICE's terms. The v0.5.0 plan's candidate section is rewritten to that specification: the repository at a pinned commit (7e0078a today), `twoline2rv` behind the corpus's harness, the recipe in `harnesses/`, the row labelled as CelesTrak's reference code; the archive fetch is dropped. For #169 itself: the reference's `satnum` is now an `int`, which holds a nine-digit number, so the maintainer's path to the nine-digit case runs through the code he is about to copy; nothing filed, the owner's call.
