**D-203 (2026-10-02) — v0.5.0 item 1, reproducible package builds (owner instruction): `tools/stage_package.py` refuses to build without the build time as `SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH` (an argument or the environment), stamps every staged file and folder with that time and the modes 0644 and 0755, passes the variable to `python -m build`, and repacks both archives so that each depends on its members' names and bytes alone; two builds of the same export with the same epoch, seconds apart and under umasks 022 and 077, are byte-identical. Built and tested, not committed, not released; the version is unchanged.** The plan's two open questions, settled by four baseline builds before any change [tested: build 1.6.1, setuptools 84.0.0, Python 3.14.6, the export of public b1e100b]: setuptools' wheel writer honours `SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH` for the zip entries' times and for nothing else, the entries' modes following the builder's umask (103 of 105 entries differed between umasks 022 and 077 with the epoch set); the sdist's writer ignores it entirely and records each member's staging time, the builder's uid, user and group names and the umask, and the build time and the file name in the gzip header (140 of 140 members differed). Normalising the staged tree fixed the sdist's members and not the wheel's modes: the frontend builds the wheel from the sdist it unpacks into a temporary tree under the umask, and 18 entries still differed by mode alone. So both archives are repacked, the plan's step 4 extended from the sdist to the wheel by that measurement: the sdist's members sorted, each dated the epoch, owner 0:0 with empty names, mode 0644 or 0755, GNU format, gzip with the epoch as its header time and no file name; the wheel's entries sorted, dated the epoch, mode 0644, deflated, the contents and RECORD untouched; an epoch before 1980, which a zip entry cannot hold, is refused. After that, builds under umask 022 and 077 two seconds apart give the same wheel (SHA-256 f8fb60c8...) and the same sdist (afccb312...) [tested]; the audit reports 0 problems for both; each installs into a clean environment and runs `gpconf run --preset reference` offline with 5 cases passing and 13 needing provider data, and the 80 corpus files installed match the export manifest [tested]. Tests, five new in `tests/test_stage_package.py`: the epoch from the argument or the environment as an integer; the staged tree stamped under umask 077; the repacked archives identical across two umasks and two moments, their members' fields checked, the wheel's contents untouched and still passing the audit; a build refused with exit 2 and nothing staged when the epoch is absent; and the real double build, which runs only where the build frontend and setuptools are importable and is skipped in the runner's environment and in CI (suite 295 OK, 7 skipped; the module 19 OK in the build environment, the double build included) [tested]. For the release: the checklist's build step passes `--source-date-epoch "$(git log -1 --format=%ct v0.5.0)"`, the dry run before the tag is compared with the build from the tag by SHA-256, the release notes name the toolchain and the epoch, and earlier releases are not rebuilt. Recorded, not assumed: identity across machines (another Python patch version, OS or zlib) is untested [untested]; the deflate and gzip bytes depend on zlib, so the claim is "the same toolchain on the same platform" until a build of a tag in CI is compared with the owner's.

**D-204 (2026-10-02) — First report to CelesTrak's project, on the owner's word: https://github.com/CelesTrak/fundamentals-of-astrodynamics/issues/172 (01:19:24Z, through the repository's bug template, which labelled it bug), on the Alpha-5 decoder of `twoline2rv` in the C++ at 7e0078a: the letters I and O, which the scheme never uses, decode to 0, so `O1234` gives `satnum` 1234 and `I0000` gives 0 with `error` 0, and a non-digit after the letter, `AA000`, throws `std::invalid_argument` out of the function; and one line on python-sgp4 #169 pointing at it (01:20:27Z, issuecomment-5943802630), since that maintainer is about to copy the code. Both bodies read back identical [tested: GitHub's API; the issue's title and each of its six fields verbatim].** Why now and not with the row: D-201's findings were private pending a report; python-sgp4's maintainer wrote on #169 that he will copy CelesTrak's current C++ this month, so a report before the copy lets a fix reach what he copies and spares him patching twice; the hand-run row of CelesTrak's reference stays deferred out of v0.5.0 (owner, 2026-10-02). The channel: the repository's README invites issues and its CONTRIBUTING triages them through the templates, with a pull request made to wait for an acknowledgment; its own #163 and #164, an outsider's, were fixed by the maintainer within six weeks; so an issue, not mail, a discussion or a pull request. The text, under the outreach convention (D-186) inside the template's fields: one finding leading, one reproduction (a twelve-line program with the O1234 set, compiled and run as posted before filing, I0000 and AA000 run the same way [tested]), one number; Space-Track's definition as the authority in Expected Behavior and the corpus's vectors not cited, since they are downstream of it (owner); no offer to run the corpus against the C++ (owner: first contact, it would read as a pitch); lowercase acceptance left out as a leniency rather than a defect and kept in the private note; the C# and MATLAB decoders of the same commit named as not run. The reproduction is kept as `batch-g/vallado/repro-issue.cpp`. The #169 line, one sentence with the link, posted after the issue existed so that the link was live (D-193's order). D-201 takes the link in place, the one edit the Batch G convention allows. Open: CelesTrak's triage; whether the C# and MATLAB decoders share the table.

**D-205 (2026-10-02) — v0.5.0 item 2, the runner side (owner instruction): a vectors command may answer `{"unsupported": "<reason>"}` and a Python hook may raise `gpconf.runner.Unsupported`, and the hook's item then skips with the reason, as it does for an adapter without the hook; an operation is unsupported for all of its vectors or none, and a mix fails the item; any non-zero exit of a vectors command stays a rejection, exit 3 included; an unsupported answer without a reason is malformed. Built and tested, not committed; the three shipped vector harnesses do not yet give the answer, and their adoption and re-runs are the next step, on the owner's word.** Why JSON and not exit 3: in the vectors protocol any non-zero exit already means a rejection, so giving 3 a meaning would turn existing adapters' rejections into skips, while the JSON answer was malformed before (it failed under D-118) and so changes no valid answer's meaning; a test exits 3 with the unsupported answer and is still rejected. Additive under the README's rule: every existing answer keeps its meaning, a harness that does not adopt the answer gets today's results, and the three built-in Python adapters raise nothing new [tested: 305 tests OK, 7 skipped, the tests that pin the built-in adapters' results among them]. The change, in `gpconf/runner.py`: `CommandParser._vec` reads the exit code first, then `unsupported` (a non-empty string, else the answer is malformed), then `error`, then `result`; `run_vectors` wraps each hook in `_HookCalls`, which counts the vectors the hook declares unsupported, the valid-vector loops pass over such a vector, and `_vector_item` writes the item: skip with "parser reports <hook> unsupported: <reason>" when every vector was declared unsupported, fail with the count when only some were, else pass or fail on the vectors as before; the skip for a missing hook is unchanged. Why all or none: a library has the operation or not, and an adapter answering unsupported for the vectors it cannot handle and results for the rest would hide failures behind a skip. `docs/ADAPTERS.md`: the protocol paragraph rewritten and a note under the hooks table; its tle.js sentence still says `alpha5_encode` answers an error and the item fails, true of the shipped harness until the next step rewords it with the harness. Tests, ten in `tests/test_vector_unsupported.py`: the Python raise skips the item with the reason and the case passes; a partial declaration fails with the count; an empty reason skips with "no reason given"; any other raise still fails its vector (D-118); an adapter without the hook skips as before; the command answer raises `Unsupported` with the reason; exits 1, 2 and 3 are rejections whatever the answer says; an empty or non-string reason is malformed; error, result and empty answers keep their meaning; end to end through `--vectors-cmd`, a command lacking two operations skips their two items and passes the other three. What moves when the harnesses adopt it, from the plan's table of the round-two reports: tle.js's `alpha5-encode`, `ccsds-epoch-strings` and `catalog-number-is-integer` items and libsgp4's and astroz's `alpha5-encode` from fail to skip, no case count and no site figure. Noticed, not changed: a ResourceWarning for an unclosed `manifest.json` handle at `runner.py:439` under verbose test runs, older than this change.

**D-206 (2026-10-02) — v0.5.0 item 2, the harness side: the three shipped vector harnesses answer unsupported for the operations their libraries do not have, tle.js's for `alpha5_encode`, `parse_epoch` and `parse_catalog_id`, libsgp4's and astroz's for `alpha5_encode`; re-run at their pinned versions, each moves exactly the items the plan named from fail to skip and nothing else, no case status and no count; and the five libraries that pass no vectors command, re-run the same way at the owner's instruction rather than reasoned about, are identical to their round-two reports item for item. Built, run and recorded, not committed.** The edits: `gpconf/adapters/tlejs_vectors.mjs` (the two error answers become unsupported answers, the header says so; the guide's excerpt of lines 8 to 34 regenerated and its tle.js sentence reworded), `harnesses/libsgp4/vectors.cpp` (an explicit `alpha5_encode` branch answering unsupported with exit 0, the unknown-op error kept) and `harnesses/astroz/vectors.zig` (the one answer), with their private copies updated for the test that pins them [tested: `tests/test_harnesses.py`, `tests/test_adapters_doc.py`; suite 305 OK, 7 skipped]. The runs, 2026-10-02, in fresh scratch environments built from the recipes' pinned commits (libsgp4 c6ebb90, astroz 67ca74c with Zig 0.16.0, Gpredict c9fa018, gods-eye-view ce671ce with satellite.js 6.0.2, SatDump f3d82ad and 1.2.2 with the private stand-ins, tle.js 5.0.3 and satellite.js 7.1.0 in a Node project, PyEphem 4.2.1 in its own environment), the runner at the private tree after D-205, each compared item by item with its latest round-two report by the notes' `compare_reports.py` [tested: eleven reports, logs and comparisons kept as `report-*-d206.json`, `run-*-d206.txt` and `compare-*-d206.txt` in each library's `round-2/` folder, scratchpad paths neutralised; the script as `batch-g/rerun-all-d206.sh`]: tle.js fields and api, 186 items each, 183 identical, `alpha5-encode`, `ccsds-epoch-strings` and `catalog-number-is-integer` fail to skip with the harness's reasons; libsgp4, 244 items, 243 identical, `alpha5-encode` fail to skip; astroz fields and jd, 240 items each, 239 identical, `alpha5-encode` fail to skip; satellite.js 239 of 239 identical, PyEphem 189 of 189, Gpredict 189 of 189, gods-eye-view 179 of 179, SatDump master 215 of 215 and release 167 of 167. No case status moved in any run: the vector case still fails for tle.js (`alpha5-decode`, `two-digit-year-pivot`) and for libsgp4 and astroz (`ccsds-epoch-strings`, `catalog-number-is-integer`), as the plan said, so no count and no site figure moves and the rows stay as published; when a row whose run used a vectors command is next published, its S-entry says that the vector items for operations the library does not have skip from corpus 0.5.0, a change in the corpus's protocol and not in the library. Why the five were run: the plan's "cannot be affected" was a reading of the change, true until it is not; the runs make it a measurement, and since the environments were rebuilt from the recipes rather than restored, the identity also re-confirms every recipe at its commit on this machine's toolchain (Apple clang 21, Zig 0.16.0, Node 26.5.0). Not changed: the three built-in Python adapters, which have no operation to declare unsupported, and the private round-two findings, which stand.

**D-207 (2026-10-02) — v0.5.0 item 3, the gate's counts to the split definition (D-194's ruling, owner instruction): `dropped` is the expected records left after loaded, refused and misidentified, never below 0; `ids_not_returned` keeps the count `dropped` carried before, the expected ids no record came back under; `extra` counts misidentified records beyond the expected records left, so a surplus is reported rather than subtracted below zero; the four outcomes sum to the expected count plus `extra`; the headline is built from the split and names the surplus. Built and tested, not committed.** The change, in `gpconf/runner.py`: the values item's counts (one site; the empty-answer path already counted the split, and the corrupt-input path counts one outcome per record with its own surplus since D-175), with the comment over them rewritten; in `gpconf/gates.py`: the docstring states the 0.5.0 definition, `format_result` carries `ids_not_returned` and `extra` into the gate's JSON, `_numbers` prints the surplus as "N of them beyond the file's records", and the branch for a parser that loads every expected record prints the same numbers as the others instead of `loaded` alone, since that branch hid a surplus behind "256 loaded"; for every existing report the text there is unchanged, "256 loaded" being what the numbers give when nothing else is counted [tested: the three built-in reports regenerated, FAILURES.md differing in its timestamp line alone]. What moves, checked against the tree and the eight libraries' latest reports (the D-206 runs) rather than the plan: the runner's headline for five runs, PyEphem 4.2.1, satellite.js 7.1.0, Gpredict 2.6 and tle.js 5.0.3 in both presets from "256 misidentified, 256 dropped silently" (or "(refusals not reported by this adapter)") to "256 misidentified", and gods-eye-view ce671ce from "1 misidentified, 256 dropped (refusals not reported by this adapter)" to "1 misidentified, 255 dropped (refusals not reported by this adapter)"; SatDump ("256 dropped"), libsgp4 and astroz ("256 loaded") and the three built-in adapters do not move [tested: the new arithmetic applied to each report's counts]. The site's gate lines already read the split, "256 misidentified, every catalog number read as 0" and the like, gods-eye-view's "1 misidentified (kept under the key NaN), 255 dropped as duplicates of it", so no site figure moves; the README's gate sentence already defines the split; `docs/ADAPTERS.md` now lists the two counts, defines `dropped` as the records left, and withdraws its "counted twice" sentence, replaced by the one-outcome rule with the same 256-record example (256 misidentified, 0 dropped, 256 ids_not_returned; before 0.5.0, 256 dropped as well). The private round-two reports keep the headlines computed at their time; the counts behind them are the measurement, and the new reading of them is the table above. Tests, four in `tests/test_gates.py` over the alpha5-tle-derived snapshot through the runner and the gate: every record under id 0 counts 256 misidentified, 0 dropped, 256 ids_not_returned and the headline "256 misidentified"; one record for 256 counts 1 misidentified and 255 dropped; every record right plus ten under ids the file does not hold counts 256 loaded, 10 misidentified and 10 extra, 0 dropped, with the headline naming them; the reference sums to 256 with nothing extra; in each, loaded plus misidentified plus refused_matched plus dropped equals expected plus extra. The existing tests that pin counts and words pass unchanged, `test_refusals`' "256 dropped silently" for refusals without a reason among them, since no record came back there [tested: suite 309 OK, 7 skipped]. Within a minor release, as D-194 said: the gate is a headline and never a check, no `expected.json` schema changes, and the changelog says that `dropped` changed its value for parsers that misidentify and where the old count went. For the site, from 0.5.0 a row can quote the runner's headline as it stands, which S-059 and S-060 could not.

**D-208 (2026-10-02) — v0.5.0 prepared in the private repository (owner instruction, release date 2026-10-01): the version constants, the README, the changelog's 0.5.0 section, the adapter guide's sample header, the regenerated manifest, case documents, built-in reports and failure catalogue, and the suite in three environments; the checklist `docs/handoff/release-checklist-v0.5.0.md`, worked from the tree rather than the plan note. **D-216 (2026-10-03) — The Vallado harness built as a recipe, `harnesses/vallado/` (owner instruction; the candidate the v0.5.0 plan deferred, D-198 and D-201): CelesTrak's fundamentals-of-astrodynamics, the SGP4 C++ folder alone, pinned at 4b04ddc, nothing vendored; run against corpus v0.5.0's eighteen cases, 8 of 18 fail. No site row, no site entry, nothing published (owner). Findings about the reader were recorded (D-134), and nothing is reported to CelesTrak while #172 is unanswered (owner).** The source: `software/cpp/SGP4/SGP4/SGP4.cpp` and `SGP4.h` (sha256 7e984e29… and 0563e6e8…), from GitHub by a sparse clone; nothing requested from celestrak.org. The pin, and why it is not the commit first named: the owner named 7e0078a10e9aed2a44eef0ab646c0018ca69e1ad (2026-08-31), the commit that last changed `SGP4.cpp`, for the folder's carve-out from the AGPL. Read strictly, the carve-out is not in the tree at that commit: its tree has no NOTICE and its README names the AGPL-3.0 alone. The NOTICE that says the SGP4 C++ source "retains those original unrestricted terms" of AIAA 2006-6753, the README's exception paragraph and the folder's own licensing lines were added by 4b04ddce172a3f5d958b4256a29ea996c8e666be on 2026-09-28. The two files compiled have the same git blob ids at 7e0078a, at 4b04ddc and on `main` at 49df047 [tested]. So the recipe pins 4b04ddc (owner, on that finding): the same code, with its terms in the checkout the recipe makes; the README names 7e0078a as the commit of the code. The run was made at both, from a fresh clone each time: the same 187 items, status and text [tested]. The recipe copies nothing of the repository: the harness is the corpus's own code. The harness: TLE and 2LE through `SGP4Funcs::twoline2rv()`, the code's only reader; each line handed over as the file carries it, an `elsetrec` that starts zeroed, the arguments python-sgp4's wrapper passes; the record is what the reader left in the structure, units turned back, the epoch the Julian date it stored; an exception the reader lets out, and the structure's error flag when the call leaves it non-zero, come back through the refusal channel with the reader's own reason (D-144, as Gpredict's harness does for its module's own check); each line 1 with whatever follows it (D-183); the vector hooks through the same call, with `alpha5_encode`, `parse_epoch` and `parse_catalog_id` answered unsupported (D-205), the code having no writer and no OMM reader. Result [tested: the run's report, 187 items, the same at `-O0` and `-O1`]: 3 pass, 8 fail, 6 skip, 1 not exercised; the gate reads "256 loaded" for the Alpha-5 rendering; every core value of every valid record matches the frozen values, the epoch exactly and the elements within 4e-16 relative, the Alpha-5 fields of the 604 derived lines and all 64 valid decode vectors included. The decode item fails on invalid fields the reader accepts, among them the two reported as CelesTrak/fundamentals-of-astrodynamics#172 (D-204). Findings about the reader were recorded (D-134); the specifics, the probes and the runs are private, in `docs/handoff/batch-g/vallado/recipe-run/`. Owner decisions of the same day: a row, when it exists, reads 8 of 18, its breakdown saying that six of the eight fail on one of the recorded findings; not now. Nothing goes to CelesTrak while #172 sits unanswered; one of the recorded findings warrants a report of its own when the time comes, for reasons kept with it. Open question, listed in the handoff: this harness reports the reader's error flag as a refusal, and the corpus's `sgp4` preset ignores the same flag when python-sgp4's accelerated class returns it for the same input; one of the two is wrong. Changed: `harnesses/vallado/` (README, `harness.cpp`, `vectors.cpp`, `common.h`), `harnesses/README.md` and `harnesses/.gitignore`, the README's row for `harnesses/`, `tests/test_harnesses.py` (the sixth recipe, its private copies, and the pinned commit in the build command). Not changed: the site, any case, any expected value, the changelog. Alternatives: the pin at 7e0078a with the NOTICE's commit named beside it (the first form of the recipe; not kept: a checkout made by the recipe would then hold no statement of the terms the recipe relies on); the run through python-sgp4's copy, as D-197 did (not what was asked: its wrapper adjusts what goes in and what comes out, so it is not an independent reading of the reference); the reader's error flag carried as a value and not as a refusal (the open question).

**D-217 (2026-10-03) — The second audit, decided (owner): a narrow audit by a named human, not yet; the grader first, the answer key second, a sample of the hand-run recipes third, with the live-tier limit stated in the audit's own scope; sought as two small asks rather than one large one. It answers the validation half of JOSS's required assertion and not the design-decisions half.** Where things stand: `AUDIT.md` covered v0.1.0 and was performed by a separate AI session, not a human, as the README says; nothing since has been separately audited. Since that release the code under `gpconf/`, `tools/` and `tests/` has changed by about 8,900 lines and the fixtures by about 28,000, with two cases added [tested: `git diff --shortstat v0.1.0` on the public repository, 2026-10-03]. What the audit covers, in this order. (1) The grader: the runner's judgement of a parser, that is the fail-closed rule, the tolerances, the refusal channel, the gate's counts and the unsupported answer, read and attacked with deliberately wrong parsers. It comes first because most of the code written since v0.1.0 is there, the first audit recomputed values and looked least at it, and the fixes of v0.2.1 were all cases of the corpus reporting a success it had not earned. (2) The answer key: the expected values of the cases added or regenerated since v0.1.0, recomputed with a reader that is not the corpus's. (3) A sample of the recipes: two or three of the hand-run libraries rebuilt from their recipes and run, since those results name other projects. Out of scope: the site, the prose, the decision log. The live-tier limit, stated in the audit's scope and not left for the auditor to discover: the live-tier expected values were frozen from provider responses of 2026-09-21 that the corpus does not redistribute, so an outside auditor can re-fetch and hash-check the stable-tier sources but cannot recompute the live-tier values unless shown those bytes; the audit is scoped to the stable and derived data unless that is first settled with the provider. Shape: a named human with standing in the field, the report published under their name; two small asks to two people (recompute a set of stable records with a reader of your own; try to make a wrong parser pass) rather than a week asked of one. Not yet: the surface moved five releases in two weeks, an audit should be of the release it will be cited for, and a reviewer is more likely to come from use than from a cold request. What it answers and what it does not: JOSS requires a submitting author to assert that human authors "reviewed, edited, validated all AI-assisted outputs and made the core design decisions"; an independent human audit is the strongest answer to the validation half, and is the engagement beyond the author that JOSS's single-author check looks for; it does nothing for the design-decisions half, which is a fact about the past that only a reading of the record settles. A further AI audit is worth running before a release as hygiene, and is not this. Alternatives: an audit of every surface (not taken: two to three weeks of a reviewer's time by estimate, most of it of low value); a second AI audit presented as the answer (not taken: it adds nothing outside the project); an audit now, of v0.5.0 (not taken: stale by the time it matters). Nothing is asked of anyone by this entry: the owner makes the asks.

**D-218 (2026-10-03) — Reversal of one choice in D-216: the Vallado harness no longer reports the reader's error flag as a refusal. The flag is carried in the record as `_error`, with a note on standard error; the `sgp4` preset, which ignores the same flag from python-sgp4, was right, and the open question is closed (owner ruling on the agent's analysis).** What the flag is [tested: source read at the pin]: `satrec.error` is the propagation model's return code on the elements. `SGP4.cpp` documents it in the headers of `sgp4init()` and `sgp4()` ("return code - non-zero on error", codes 1 to 6) and not in `twoline2rv()`'s, whose one documented output is the structure; `twoline2rv()` resets it at its top, and it is set when `sgp4init()` runs `sgp4()` at time 0. python-sgp4 documents its attribute as the "Error code (1-6) documented in sgp4()". Why it is not the reader's refusal [tested: a probe on the reader, with the caller's structure in three states]: for the corrupt-input case's line 1 with no line 2 after it, the flag is 2 when the structure starts zeroed, as the harness's does, and 0 when it does not, while the reader does the same thing each time and reports nothing. So the refusal D-216's harness reported was made by the harness's own zeroed structure, and the item it passed credited the reader with a check it does not make. A refusal in this corpus is the reader declining its input: an exception, or the reader's own return, as Gpredict's is (D-178); a propagator's status that depends on what the caller's memory held is a value. Changed: `harnesses/vallado/harness.cpp` (the flag no longer refused), `vectors.cpp` (the flag no longer answered as an error) and the recipe's README. Run again at the pin, at `-O0` and `-O1` [tested]: 186 items where D-216's run had 187; the item for the line 1 with no line 2 after it goes from pass to fail, a record built from the corrupt input, and that file's refusals item is gone; nothing else moves; 8 of 18 as before, the corrupt-input case now failing on four of its inputs. **D-223 (2026-10-03) — The finding of D-179 reported to gods-eye-view as issue #906, on the owner's word; the owner's hold of 2026-09-27 on it lifted.** The hold was until #751 or pull request #767 closed, a third thread on that repository the same day being too much. The owner lifted it on 2026-10-03: eleven days had passed with no maintainer reply on either, and the finding reproduces on v0.2.1 (D-221). The report was drafted and filed after the rebase of #767 was up (D-222), so that it arrived alongside work and not ahead of it. The finding, which this log can now state: the application's TLE reader takes a group in fixed steps of three lines, so one set that is missing a line puts every later set out of step and each is skipped; the rest of the group is dropped, and nothing reports it. Eight sets with the second one's line 2 removed give one entry where the whole text gives eight [tested: the report's own reproduction, run on a clean checkout of v0.2.1 before filing]; the corpus's missing-line file followed by five more sets leaves one catalog entry where the unedited text leaves eight, the layer logs the group as loaded with one satellite, and the application's `next_satellite_pass` tool says that a satellite which is in the text is not in the group [tested: the recipe's harness and a probe at v0.2.1, D-221; first at ce671ce, D-179]. The loop is `src/sources/tle.js:12` since v0.2.0; its own test covers a broken block of three lines, which keeps the step. The text: under the outreach convention, through the repository's bug form, which is the only way in and has seven required fields; the title keeps the form's "bug: " prefix (owner). Two changes by the owner to the draft: the field that asks for the setup check's output says it will be run and pasted if wanted, where the draft only declined; and one sentence offers a patch. The owner's reason for the second: the convention's stop at the number is for a first report to a stranger, and this repository already holds the owner's pull request and a confirmed issue. The report names the corpus's corrupt-input case and nothing of its counts; its element lines are the application's own test fixture with the catalog number varied. Not a duplicate [tested: the application's known-issues document and searches of its tracker, before filing]. Filed 2026-10-03T22:37:39Z from the owner's account: https://github.com/bilawalsidhu/gods-eye-view/issues/906, labelled `bug` by the form; the stored title and all seven fields equal the final form [tested: the API]. D-179 and D-221 take the link in place, the one edit the Batch G convention allows. The site: with this report filed, the condition the site's S-056 sets for the gods-eye-view row is met; the row can move to the v0.2.1 run (D-221) with #906 as its second-round report, under its own entry and on the owner's word. It is not moved by this entry. Open: the maintainers' answer; the patch, if they want it.

**D-224 (2026-10-03) — What gods-eye-view's count rests on, said in the recipe's README (owner instruction): five of its seven failing cases fail on the mean motion, a property of the application's dependency, satellite.js 6.0.2, and not a fault of the application. The first count, 6 of 17, rested on the same value for five of its six, and no public text said so.** How it came up: before the site row moved (site S-079) the owner asked what the count rests on beyond the row's two reports. The run's report (D-221) answers: in five of the seven failing cases the values items fail on `mean_motion`, and two of those five also carry a failing `records-returned` item for a name-less 2LE file, from which the application's reader, taking three-line sets only, returns nothing. Why the value differs [tested: the source of the 6.0.2 the run installed]: that version's `sgp4init` overwrites `satrec.no` with the model's own mean motion and keeps no copy of the printed one; the harness returns what the application's record holds, so it cannot return the printed value. The library's 7.1.0 keeps the printed value apart, and the corpus's `satellite.js` preset reads that copy (`gpconf/adapters/satellitejs.mjs`), which is why the library's own run does not fail on it. A diagnostic [tested: kept privately with the run, `docs/handoff/batch-g/gods-eye-view/v0.2.1/`]: the same run with each record given the mean motion its line 2 prints, and nothing else changed, gives 4 pass, 2 within tolerance and 4 fail of the 18: `analyst-objects`, `supgp-celestrak-classification-c` and `bstar-and-derivative-forms` pass, so those three fail on the mean motion alone; `epoch-year-19xx` and `baseline-iss-five-formats` still fail, on the 2LE file alone; the Alpha-5 case and the corrupt-input case fail as before. The diagnostic is not a result: the count stays 7 of 18, what the corpus measured through the recipe as shipped. The other two failing cases: the Alpha-5 case, on the collapse reported as #751 (D-136); and the corrupt-input case, on the application's own reader for the set with no line 2 and the set after it (#906, D-223), and on satellite.js's reader for a line one character short, the lineage's design (D-215), and for the letter O in the epoch field, reported to satellite.js as #190 (D-176). Changed: one paragraph in `harnesses/gods-eye-view/README.md`. Not changed: the harness, any count, any expected value; D-136, D-179 and D-221, whose counts stand and which this entry reads. The site says the same in the row's breakdown (S-079). Alternatives: the harness taking the mean motion from line 2 itself (rejected: it would report a value the application does not hold, and the recipe measures the application through its own path); a status of its own for a dependency's property (not built: one sentence where the count is stated serves one library); leaving it unsaid (rejected: five of seven failing cases would read as the application's).

**D-228 (2026-10-03) — v0.5.1, first of its items: the fetch stops at the first response its list does not expect, a refusal above all; such a response is kept beside the data and is never read as data; and after a refusal no request is made for two hours (owner instruction, the two additions approved with it).** Found on 2026-10-03, when the corpus's description of its own fetch was checked against the code. What was wrong, in two parts. First, the fetch list's `allow_error` flag sat on 21 of its 64 entries, 18 of them in a user's run of 61, and let any HTTP error pass on them: the 404 it was written for, and equally a 403, a 5xx or a refused redirect. Six of the 21 are recorded as answering 404; the other fifteen answered 200 when captured. CelesTrak's usage policy asks machine-to-machine software to stop querying on any response that is not an HTTP 200 and to have a person look, and its GP formats page says a 403 or 404 will not change by repeating the request (as recorded in `docs/RESEARCH.md` §3 and §11.1; neither page was re-read for this entry). Second, a response the fetch did not expect was written to the data path itself, "so it is never re-requested", and the runner then read it as provider data: with a stand-in refusal page in place of `analyst.csv` the reference adapter failed the case, "reference reader found 0 of 565 expected records" [tested 2026-10-03 with a stand-in page, no request made], a failure charged to whatever parser was under test. What changed. (1) `allow_error` is gone; an entry recorded as answering 404 carries `"expect_status": 404`, and a test holds the list to the manifest. The run goes on past a response only when it is an HTTP 200 that is neither an HTML page nor empty, or the expected 404 with the provider's no-data text (`No GP data found`, `No SupGP data found`). Anything else ends the run at once with exit status 2: a 403 or 429 on any entry, a 404 where data is expected, a 404 with another body, a redirect, a 5xx, a 2xx that is not 200. Nothing is retried and no further request is made. A network failure still exits 3 and keeps nothing. (2) The response is kept as `<file>.unexpected`, with its metadata as `<file>.unexpected.meta.json`, beside the data and never in its place; if the file was already on disk, a refused refresh leaves the earlier data standing. The fetch treats the URL as answered: a later run does not ask again, short of `--force` two hours or more after that answer, and an expected answer then removes the kept one. (3) After a refusal, HTTP 403 or 429, no request at all is made for two hours, with or without `--force`, and the dry run says the same; without this each re-run would ask the next URL of the list and collect one more refusal. A kept response is the only record this needs: no marker file. (4) The runner reports a file whose last answer was kept this way as `not-fetched`, with the status and time the fetch recorded and no fetch command, since a plain fetch does not ask again; the case's JSON carries it under `unexpected`, and a line under the count line says how many files are missing for that reason. It also refuses to read a response that a fetch before this change saved in the data's place: a provider file whose metadata records a status other than 200 and other than the one the manifest records for it is `not-fetched`, not parsed. The recorded 404 bodies are untouched by all of this: they are the empty answers the corpus hands to a parser (D-143). The stop message says what to do: on a refusal, read the kept response, which says why; otherwise report it to the corpus, since the list may be out of date. Consequences accepted: thirteen entries of a user's run that answered 200 at the capture lose their tolerance, so an endpoint whose answer changes for good stops each user's run once at that URL until a release updates the list; that is the policy's stop-and-report working as meant. The fetch's metadata now names `tools/fetch.py v3`. Alternatives: keeping the flag and narrowing it to 404 (rejected: a 404 with another body, or on an entry that expects data, is still not the answer the list knows); leaving unexpected responses on the data path with a marker in their metadata (rejected: a file whose metadata is lost would be read as data again); a marker file for the two quiet hours (not needed: the kept response records the status and the time). Not changed: which URLs are requested, the pause, the two-hour rule for `--force`, re-captures, stable-tier reuse, the drift check, any case, check or expected value. Tests (`tests/test_fetch_stop.py`, 21, the real request function against a stand-in opener with socket creation blocked): the known 404 passes; a 403 stops the run on a strict entry and on one that expects a 404; a 404 with another body, a 404 where data is expected, a 500, a redirect, a 204, an HTML page and an empty body under 200 each stop it; no answer exits 3; the kept response is not asked again, is with `--force` after two hours, and a good answer clears it; a refused refresh keeps the earlier data; two quiet hours hold for every form of the command and end on time; a 429 is a refusal and a 500 is not; the list agrees with the manifest; the runner reports a kept response and an old in-place one as `not-fetched`, never as a failure, and still reads the recorded 404. Suite 331 OK. Not covered by a test: a real refusal from CelesTrak, which no test may provoke.

**D-229 (2026-10-03) — v0.5.1, second item: the ten launch-window requests leave the fetch; a provider file that no fetch requests is reported as `not-available`; and wherever the corpus states a count it now says the count was measured with launch-window files that a new user's fetch cannot obtain (owner instruction; no library is re-run).** What was wrong. The fetch list asked for the Starlink G15-27 post-deployment file in five formats and for the launch nominal 799501621 in five, and stopped the run on an error from nine of the ten. The corpus's own case documents call both perishable ("nominals exist only for roughly 5-8 days after a launch", "this post-deployment file is replaced after cataloguing"), and the site's tracker counted 27 nine-digit ids in the Starlink supplemental file up to 2026-09-24 and none on 2026-09-25 [tested: the tracker's history]. So a fetch built on those requests stops for every user once the window has closed. Whether the ten still answer was not tested, on the owner's ruling: the fetch is to stop depending on the answer. Nor had anything shown it: no first-time fetch had been made since the captures of 2026-09-21, the release checks running offline [the three release checklists; the fetch tests stand a stub in for the request]. What changed. (1) The ten entries carry `"launch_window": "Starlink G15-27, launched 2026-09-20"` and stay in the list as the record of the corpus's own captures. No run plans one, under any flag, and each run says so in a line. A user's run is 51 requests and 12,511,746 bytes, with four expected 404s; a later corpus version requests 29, the 22 stable files being copied. A capture that is on disk, as in the maintainer's clone, is still compared with the manifest. (2) The runner learns those ten paths from the fetch list, which ships with the corpus, and reports a missing one as `source-present: not-available`, naming the launch and giving no fetch command. `not-fetched` would send the user to a fetch that does not bring the file, and `not-exercised` is kept for data that was read and lacked the feature (D-148). A case takes the status when none of its provider files can come from a fetch; it ranks below `not-fetched`, so a case with anything left to fetch says that. The table gains an `n/a` column, the count line "N not available", the JSON counts a `not-available` key, and the Action's summary the same count. Below the count line the runner names the case that cannot run and the cases judged on their other files, and says that published counts were measured with those files. A file that an earlier fetch saved in a capture's place, the provider's no-data answer once the window had closed, is `not-available` too and is not read. (3) Which cases, in the public copy: `supgp-celestrak-classification-c` names all five of its provider files among the ten and cannot run from a fetch at all; `nine-digit-supgp-launch-nominals` names five of six and runs on the full Starlink supplemental file alone, where its nine-digit check says `not-exercised` outside a launch window and the others (values, integer ids, classification C, the extra columns) still run; `csv-json-omitted-mandatory-fields` and `omm-xml-schema` name two each and run on the rest. `mean-motion-derivative-convention` and `tle-vs-omm-precision-loss` list the post-deployment pair among their sources but open it only on the maintainer's copy, the supplemental pair being withheld from the public one (D-049). What is lost to a user: a supplemental record in five formats, with element set 0, the 72000-series ids and the TLE epoch of a SupGP record, and a nine-digit id in JSON, XML and KVN. (4) The published counts. Every count the corpus has published was measured on the maintainer's copy, captures included. For the built-in presets a run from a user's fetch shows 16 cases passing for the reference adapter where 17 are published, 14 failing for the naive adapter where 15 are, and 6 for python-sgp4 where 8 are [tested 2026-10-03, in the maintainer's tree and in an export alike, against a stand-in for a user's data folder: every provider file but the ten captures and the three re-captures; the full Starlink file with and without its nine-digit rows gives the same three counts]. No hand-run library was run again (owner). For six of the eight hand-run rows the failing cases include `supgp-celestrak-classification-c` [the kept reports], so a fresh fetch reproduces at least one case fewer for each; tle.js's row does not include it, and SatDump's row is still the first-round one. In place of re-runs, one sentence stands wherever a count appears: the README, `docs/FAILURES.md`, the recipes' index and each recipe's README, and the runner's own output; the site's pages follow under their own decision. A finding made on the way, about the counts and not about this change: a reader that takes no row of CelesTrak's supplemental CSV fails the two pair cases on the maintainer's copy and passes them in the public copy on the same data, because the item that fails is the supplemental pair's own file, which the public copy does not open [tested with a stand-in reader: five failing cases against three]. libsgp4's published count holds two cases of that kind, and SatDump master's second-round count two [the kept reports], so those four were never reproducible from the public corpus, with or without the captures. Not changed by this entry: any case, check or expected value, and any published count; the generated `MANIFEST.md` and `case.md` files, whose sources in `tools/cases.py` now state the above, are regenerated with the release. Alternatives: keeping `not-fetched` and changing only its sentences (rejected: the count line would go on saying a case needs fetched data that no fetch brings, the kind of unearned status D-148 removed); a flag that asks for the ten again (rejected: they belong to one launch); giving the supplemental cases a source that is always served (not in a patch: it changes a case's sources, needs new captures and a re-run of every library; a candidate for a later version). Tests: `tests/test_not_available.py`, 13: the list, the plans and the runs never hold a capture; the status, its rank, the count line and the absence of a fetch command; a copy without the fetch list falls back to `not-fetched`; an old in-place answer is not read. Existing tests moved with the counts: 51 planned requests, 22 reused and 29 requested on a later version, 12 cases needing data and one not available before a fetch, 16 passing after one, and 17 with the maintainer's captures. Suite 345 OK in the maintainer's tree and 343 OK in an export.

**D-230 (2026-10-03) — v0.5.1, third item: the README's "Fetching responsibly", the fetch's help text and the quick-start comments no longer present the two-second pause as CelesTrak's policy, and the CSV default is said of the queries it belongs to (owner instruction).** What was wrong. The section opened "`tools/fetch.py` follows CelesTrak's published usage policy" and listed "2 s between requests" under it. The fetch's docstring, which `gpconf fetch --help` prints, put "requests are sequential with a fixed pause between them" under "Rules (CelesTrak usage policy …)". The quick-start comments said "~60 requests, ~12.6 MB, 2 s apart". CelesTrak's pages, as the corpus records them (`docs/RESEARCH.md` §3 and §11.1; not re-read for this entry), give a cadence for data, once per update and two hours for GP, a rule for responses, stop on anything that is not a 200, and limits, 50 errors in two hours and 100 MB a day. They publish no interval between requests. The pause is the corpus's own choice, and the corpus had been crediting it to CelesTrak since the fetch was written. What changed. The section is three things CelesTrak asks, each followed by what the fetch does about it, and then what is the corpus's own: requests one at a time with a two-second pause, no redirect followed, nothing retried, no URL twice in a run. The help text is split the same way. The quick-start comments give a user's run as the fetch list has it, 51 requests and 12.5 MB, once per corpus version, where they gave the manifest's totals, which count three maintainer-only re-captures. Two more corrections in the same paragraphs. The advice to leave out the legacy SATCAT with `--skip-case satcat-70000-cutoff` is removed: it also leaves out two first-record files filed under that case, which four other cases read [tested 2026-10-03 on the list as released in 0.5.0: the dry run then plans 54 of 61 requests; the manifest's sources]. And "CelesTrak's default format has been CSV since 2026-05-09" becomes "For GP and SupGP queries CelesTrak's default `FORMAT` has been CSV": the default is those queries', as its GP formats page and its SupGP query page say (RESEARCH §2.1), while its SATCAT queries documented JSON as theirs when read on 2026-09-20 (§2.6). The corpus relies on no default: every request names its format. Not changed: what the fetch does, which D-228 and D-229 changed; the pause itself; the changelog's earlier entries, which are history. The site's README and tracker page state the pause as the site's own rule and need nothing for this. Tests (`tests/test_fetch_text.py`, 7): the pause appears only after the sentence that calls it the corpus's own; each of the three asks is in the section; the figures the README states equal the fetch list's and the manifest's, so they cannot drift again; the expected 404s are TLE requests for objects above 99999; the old SATCAT advice is gone; the CSV default names its queries; the help text is split.

**D-231 (2026-10-03) — v0.5.1, fourth item: the legacy SATCAT file is fetched only on request, with `--include-satcat`, and a user's run becomes 50 requests and 3.1 MB (owner decision, on a point the plan raised unasked).** Why. `pub/satcat.txt` is 9,379,866 of the 12,511,746 bytes a user's run downloaded, three quarters, and one case reads it: `satcat-70000-cutoff`, a data check the runner makes on the file itself. It counts the catalog numbers, finds none at or above 70000, and reports `not-exercised` for every parser, "the parser under test was not involved" (D-129). CelesTrak's usage policy asks software to download only the data it needs (as recorded in `docs/RESEARCH.md` §11.1), and no parser needs this file. What changed. The entry carries `"opt_in": "satcat"`. A fetch leaves it out and says so in a line; `--include-satcat` brings it; `--force` does not refresh a copy that an earlier fetch left on disk unless the flag is given, so a clone that fetched under 0.5.0 does not download it again unasked, and such a copy is still compared with the manifest. A user's run is 50 requests and 3,131,880 bytes, with four expected 404s; a later corpus version requests 28. The runner: without the file the case reports `not-fetched`, as a provider file that is not on disk always has (D-148); the item names the flag; and the line under the count says that the case waits on a file the fetch brings only on request, that no parser takes part in the check, and how to fetch it. The general fetch command is no longer printed on its account, a plain fetch not bringing it. So the control run from a fresh fetch reads 16 cases passing, one needing fetched data and one not available [tested against a stand-in for a user's data folder]. With the file on disk the case reports `not-exercised`, as before. The README's fetch section gains CelesTrak's fourth ask, download only what you need, with this as what the fetch does about it. Alternatives: reporting `not-exercised` when the file is absent, so that the count line would not say that a case needs fetched data (not taken: `not-exercised` is the status of a check that was made and involved no parser, a missing provider file has been `not-fetched` since D-148, and the sentence under the count line says that nothing about the parser depends on it; it is one status and two tests if the owner prefers the other reading); putting the case's six small SATCAT records on request as well, since no parser check reads them either (not done: they are under 2 kB together); dropping the case (no: that the legacy file stops at 69999 is a fact the corpus states, and the check is what holds it). Not changed: the case, its check, its expected values. Tests (`tests/test_fetch_optin.py`, 11): one entry is on request and it is most of the bytes; a plain fetch and its dry run leave it out and say how to ask; the flag brings it; `--force` leaves a copy on disk alone unless it is asked for, and the two-hour rule holds for it; skipping the case is not the way to leave it out; the runner's item names the flag; the report says what the case waits on; before any fetch the case is one of those with nothing on disk; with the file the check is made and reports `not-exercised`. The counts in four other test files moved with it.

**D-232 (2026-10-03) — The SATCAT case reports `not-exercised` when its file is absent by default, with a line saying that `--include-satcat` runs the check; the `not-fetched` that D-231 chose is reversed (owner decision).** The owner: saying that the case needs fetched data tells every user to fetch the file that was just made optional. D-231 had the case report `not-fetched` without the legacy file, so the count line of every default run said that one case needed fetched data, and the line below it gave the command. What changes. When the legacy file is not on disk, and no kept response says that a fetch asked for it and was refused, the case's one check, `satcat-legacy-below-70000`, is recorded `not-exercised` with the detail that the data check was not made, that the fetch brings the file only on request and that the parser under test is not involved, followed by the flag, and by the command where a run names commands. The case's status is then what it is with the file, before a fetch and after one. The count line no longer counts it among the cases that need fetched data. One line under the count says that the case is a data check on a file the fetch brings only on request, that it was not made, that nothing about the parser under test depends on it, and that `--include-satcat` runs it. The JSON result carries the file and the flag under `on_request`. A file that was asked for and refused is still `not-fetched`, with what the fetch recorded (D-228). Why this is not the status D-148 took away. D-148 kept `not-exercised` from a case whose parser checks never ran for want of data, since the word would claim that the data lacked a feature when no data had been read. This case has no parser check: with the file its status is `not-exercised` because no parser is involved (D-129), which is as true without the file, and the item says in so many words that the check was not made. So the control run reads 16 cases passing, one not available and one not exercised from a fresh fetch, and 5 passing, 11 needing fetched data, one not available and one not exercised before any fetch [tested against stand-ins for both folders]. The case's six small SATCAT records are still `not-fetched` until a fetch and are counted in its `n/f` column. Not changed: the fetch (D-231), the check, the case's expected values. Tests: the runner's and the command line's in `tests/test_fetch_optin.py` rewritten and one added, 12 in the file: the status and the item with and without commands, the line and the absence of any fetch advice, the reading before a fetch, and a refusal kept for the file; the count lines in `tests/test_install_layout.py` and `tests/test_action.py`; the public clone's branch of the SATCAT test in `tests/test_runner.py`.

**D-233 (2026-10-03) — Which published counts hold a failing case that the public corpus cannot reproduce: libsgp4's, two of its nine, and SatDump master's second-round count, two of its nine, and no other. The counts stay; the two recipe READMEs say it; the site's libsgp4 row gains the clause with the release's site update (owner decision, on the finding in D-229, with the check of every other row the owner asked for).** The mechanism. The two pair cases, `mean-motion-derivative-convention` and `tle-vs-omm-precision-loss`, list the Starlink G15-27 supplemental pair among their sources. The maintainer's `expected.json` holds that pair among the case's items; the public one withholds it (D-049); and the runner opens a pair's files only where the pair is listed. So a reader that returns no record from the supplemental CSV fails those two cases on the maintainer's copy, on the fail-closed item for that file (D-144), and passes them in the public copy on the same data [tested with a stand-in reader, D-229]. The check: every kept report behind a published count was read for failing cases all of whose failing items are on that pair [the kept reports, read by a script kept privately; no library was run]. libsgp4 3.0, 9 of 18: both cases, each on the one item, the supplemental CSV of which `Tle::FromCsv()` refuses every row (reported as #45; D-182 counted three such cases, and the third, `csv-json-omitted-mandatory-fields`, fails on the nominal's own CSV, which the public copy does open). SatDump master, second round, 9 of 18: the same two, for the same reason on its side (reported in #1221; D-181 names the three). None in the others: PyEphem 4.2.1, satellite.js 7.1.0, Gpredict 2.6, gods-eye-view 0.2.1, tle.js 5.0.3 in both of its presets, astroz 0.14.0 in both of its modes, SatDump 1.2.2 in both rounds, SatDump master's first-round count, which is the one on the site, Vallado's SGP4, and the three built-in presets. The owner's ruling: a published count stays, since it was true for the data it was measured on; where it appears, the text says that two of the nine rest on the withheld supplemental pair and were never reproducible from the public corpus. Done in the corpus: `harnesses/libsgp4/README.md` and `harnesses/satdump/README.md` carry the clause beside the sentence of D-229. To do with the release's site update, under the site's own decision: the clause on the libsgp4 row. SatDump's row on the site is still the first-round one, which holds no such case; when it moves to the second round the clause goes with it. From a fetch of v0.5.1 the two effects coincide for these cases: the pair's files are launch-window captures that no fetch requests (D-229), and the public copy would not open them if it had them. Not changed: any count, any `expected.json`, what the public copy withholds. Alternative: restating the two counts as seven (rejected by the owner's ruling: the count is a measurement, and the measurement was nine).

**D-234 (2026-10-03) — v0.5.1 prepared in the private repository (owner instruction; the release date is the day it ships): the version constants, the README, the changelog's 0.5.1 section, the adapter guide's sample header, the regenerated manifest, case documents, built-in reports and failure catalogue, the suite in four environments, an export with its suite and a trial build; and the checklist, which gains a timed first-time fetch by the owner as a standing step.** A patch release under the README's rule, tooling and documentation only: D-228 to D-233. No case, check or frozen expected value changed, and the adapter protocol is as in 0.5.0. The constants: `gpconf/__init__.py`, `pyproject.toml`, `tools/make_manifest.py` and `CITATION.cff` to 0.5.1; the date fields carry 2026-10-03 and move together to the day the release ships if that is another day. The README: the status paragraph (the four items; a user's fetch at 50 requests and 3.1 MB; 15 and 8 failing cases for the naive and python-sgp4 adapters as before, 14 and 6 from a fresh fetch), both audit sentences, the Action line at `@v0.5.1`, the citation sentence, and the version-DOI sentence as a slot. The changelog: `[Unreleased]` became `[0.5.1]`, with an opening paragraph and the DOI slot. Regenerated, never edited by hand: `manifest.json`, `MANIFEST.md` and the `case.md` files, 18 cases and 148 source entries as at 0.5.0, differing from 0.5.0's by the version, the timestamp and the three gaps texts that D-229 and D-231 wrote; the three built-in reports and `docs/FAILURES.md`, whose table is unchanged. The counts under 0.5.1 on the maintainer's copy: the reference adapter 17 exact with the SATCAT check not exercised, the naive adapter 15 failing, python-sgp4 2.27 failing 8, as at 0.5.0 [tested]. Tests, exit codes checked, under Python 3.14.6 with a Node project holding satellite.js 7.1.0 and tle.js 5.0.3: the environment with python-sgp4 2.27, 364 OK (4 skipped); one with PyEphem 4.2.1, 364 OK (5 skipped); the system Python, 364 OK (8 skipped); the build environment, 364 OK (7 skipped). An export: 239 files, 5 scrubbed, 0 refused; its suite 362 OK (24 skipped); an offline reference run from it reads 5 cases passing, 11 needing fetched data, one not available and one not exercised. A trial build from the export: wheel 106 files, sdist 111, no problem found by the audit; each holds one file more than 0.5.0's, the `NOTICE` of D-219 among the licences; the trial wheel installed into a clean environment prints the same count line, and its fetch's dry run plans 50 requests. The run on Python 3.9 and 3.12 is CI's, at the push; the changed files were checked here against 3.9's grammar. The checklist's new step: after the upload, the owner runs the fetch once from the installed release into an empty cache, timed. It is the first first-time fetch since the captures of 2026-09-21, whose absence is how D-229's fault went unseen, and the only step of a release that sends requests to CelesTrak; if it stops, the stop is the finding. No hand-run library is re-run for this release (owner, D-229), so the rule of D-208's checklist, that the libraries' counts hold at a tag only if the runner differs in version strings alone, is answered otherwise: no row is restated at 0.5.1, each names the corpus version it ran against, and the sentence of D-229 says what the counts were measured with. Nothing is pushed, tagged or uploaded by this entry.

**D-238 (2026-10-04) — The letter to Dr. T.S. Kelso of CelesTrak was sent by the owner, from the owner's own address, on 2026-10-04 at about 07:53 EDT (11:53Z): the first direct word to CelesTrak's operator on how the corpus uses celestrak.org, sent before the corpus is announced to people who would run its fetch.** The sending is the owner's statement [the session did not see the message; the time is the owner's "now" read against the session's clock]. The text is the one committed privately as final on 2026-10-03, the fourth draft with the owner's two edits, 850 words, unchanged at sending (the owner's statement of 2026-10-04). What it says: who the maintainer is, and that the corpus was built with AI assistance and its first release audited by a separate AI session with no access to the build context; that no response from CelesTrak is redistributed, the corpus publishing expected values derived from the responses, each response's URL, time and SHA-256, and 604 TLE lines rendered from CSV records, with supplemental element values withheld from the public copy; how a user's fetch behaves, in the figures measured at v0.5.1's release (D-235): one run of about two minutes, 50 requests one at a time, 3.2 MB, neither the Active nor the Starlink group, the legacy SATCAT file only when asked for, a file on disk never requested again; that the fetch stops at the first non-200, keeps the answer for the user to read, retries nothing and asks nothing for two hours after a 403 or 429 (D-228), with one exception, four TLE requests for objects numbered above 99999 that are known to answer 404, of which the letter is the report to a human that his usage policy asks for; what the site's tracker requests each day, and that any other error ends its run (site S-080); the offer to stop requesting those 404s if he would rather, and to adopt a different cadence, a cap or a mirror; the three things the corpus says about his service, two read from his GP formats page and one the corpus's own observation, that his TLE rendering truncates the eccentricity where Space-Track rounds it (line 2 reproduced for 304 of 304 sets with truncation, 257 with rounding), each offered for correction; and that #172 is filed and taken up (D-236), anything further going to that tracker first. Why a letter, and why now: the fetch's requests are about to come from more addresses than the maintainer's, and the operator should hear of it from the maintainer before it shows in his logs (owner). It waited for v0.5.1 (the owner's hold of 2026-10-03) so that it describes a fetch that stops as his policy asks and does not depend on launch-window files (D-228, D-229), with figures measured and not estimated. What follows from it: from this date CelesTrak's silence is no longer unasked; a reply is logged verbatim in this log, and the public copy follows whatever it corrects; a change he asks for is made; the post that announces the corpus waits for a reply or for a week of silence, the owner's call. State at sending: #172 open, assigned, one comment, unchanged since D-236 [tested: GitHub's API, 11:53Z]; the usage policy's date as the letter gives it, 2026 May 22, is the owner's reading on the day [untested by the session, which requests nothing from celestrak.org]. The addresses and the letter's text are kept in the private handoff and are not exported.

**D-239 (2026-10-04) — A count agrees in number with the words beside it, in everything the runner, the fetch and the Action print: one case passes, fails, skips or needs fetched data where two pass, fail, skip or need it, and no line hedges a plural with a bracketed s (owner).** The slip was seen in the Action's summary at the v0.5.1 release (D-235): "1 need launch-window data that no fetch brings". The owner asked for it fixed, and for every other count joined to a fixed phrase to be checked and fixed the same way. The audit: every formatted string in `gpconf/` and `tools/action_report.py` in which a value is followed by words, 218 of them, each read in its source. Three kinds needed work. A noun hedged as "record(s)", about sixty strings: the count line's "case(s)" and the lines under it, the fetch's "request(s)", "file(s)", "capture(s)" and "minute(s)", check-tle's summary, and some forty item details. A plural noun that can meet a count of one: "expected records", "ids", "pivot vectors", "valid epoch strings", "characters", "bytes", "objects", "entries", "vectors", "fields". And a verb or a pronoun that has to follow the count: "need", "have none of their", "report", "name", "are judged on their", "are missing … for them", "were reused", "match", "differ", "remain", "were given up", "as the parser reads them". The fix, one way everywhere: `gpconf/words.py`, two functions, `qty(n, noun)` for a count with its noun and `pick(n, singular, plural)` for a word that follows it; one is singular and every other number, zero included, plural. `tools/action_report.py` carries its own three-line `pick`, since the Action's script stands alone. The status words of the count line are verbs and agree like the rest: "1 passes (exact)", "1 fails", "1 skips", "1 needs fetched data"; "not available" and "not exercised" do not change with number. Alternative: taking pass, fail and skip as labels and conjugating "need" alone (rejected: "1 fail" beside "1 needs" in one line is the same slip half mended); it is one function, `count_line`, if the owner would rather have labels. Lines built in one place were moved into functions so that a test can call them: `count_line`; the fetch's `done_line`, `dry_run_line`, `captures_line`, `refusal_line` and `drift_line`; the Action's `counts_sentence`. What does not change: no status, no count and no check, only text. A line whose counts are all other than one reads as before but for the bracketed s, so the count lines published so far stand. The JSON report's item details change in wording where they carried a bracketed plural; a later item-for-item comparison with a kept report shows that as differences of text and nothing else. `docs/FAILURES.md` is generated from those details and by `tools/make_failures.py`, which had the same form in two places ("failing item(s)", "bullet(s)"): both fixed, the three built-in adapters run again on the maintainer's copy (the reference 17 exact with the SATCAT check not exercised, 15 and 8 failing, as at 0.5.1) and the catalogue regenerated: 244 lines as before, differing in the rewording, the generated line, and four details whose cut-off point moved with the shorter text. `docs/ADAPTERS.md`'s sample line reads "0 of 1 record". Left as they are, being a maintainer's console output and not what a user's run or the Action prints: `tools/export_public.py`'s "file(s)" and `tools/stage_package.py`'s "problem(s)". Tests: `tests/test_number_agreement.py`, 22 tests, pins the singular and the plural of the count line, of each line under the table, of the fetch's five lines, of the Action's sentence, and of the item details of a shipped case whose two files hold one record and 256; and it reads the source, so that no string in the code that prints, and no line of the generated catalogue, carries a bracketed plural. Forty existing assertions moved to the new wording, and three that asserted a phrase's absence now cover both numbers. Suite 386 OK (7 skipped); the changed files pass a check against Python 3.9's grammar. No release: it ships with the next one, and nothing is exported by this entry (owner).

**D-240 (2026-10-04) — Nothing parses the count line, and so that nothing has to: the JSON report carries the case totals as numbers in a `summary` object, the README, the adapter guide and the changelog say that a script reads the report or the exit status and never the printed lines, and the catalogue's table says "(1 failing)" (owner).** The owner kept D-239's choice ("Keep the status words agreeing") and asked, before it ships, what parses the count line rather than reads it. The check, by reading each source [tested]: the scratch repository's workflow (D-158) tests the Action's numeric outputs `failed` and `exercised` and the step's outcome, and searches the JSON report for two phrases; `tools/action_report.py` counts `results[].status` in the JSON report and uses the runner's exit status, passing the printed text through unread; the corpus's CI uses exit statuses; the tests hold whole-line assertions, already in D-239's wording, and none takes a number out of a line by matching "pass", "fail" or "skip"; the tools read JSON reports; the site's jobs do not run the runner. So D-239 breaks nothing that exists. What the check found missing: the report held each case's status and its item counts as numbers but no case totals, and the README named neither `--json`, nor the exit statuses 0 and 1, nor the Action's outputs; the adapter guide described the report's fields and the three exit statuses without saying to use them in place of the printed text. Three changes, on the owner's word. (1) `summary` at the report's top level, from one function, `gpconf.runner.status_totals`: `cases`, and a count for each of the seven statuses a case can end in, zeros included, in the count line's order; the count line is written from the same numbers. Additive: every earlier field is where it was, and `tools/action_report.py` goes on counting `results`, so the Action does not depend on the new field. check-tle's report is unchanged: it lists each record with its status, and its exit status says whether any failed. (2) One sentence in the README, in the adapter guide's `--json` section and in the changelog: a script should read the JSON report or the exit status, never the printed lines, whose wording can change from one version to the next; the README adds where the numbers are, what the three exit statuses mean, and the Action's outputs. (3) `tools/make_failures.py` writes a case's failing items as "(1 failing)" where it wrote "(1 fail)": a word that does not vary with the count, and the one the site's findings table already uses. The three built-in adapters were run again on the maintainer's copy (the reference 17 exact with the SATCAT check not exercised, 15 and 8 failing, as before) and `docs/FAILURES.md` regenerated, differing in the table's cells and the generated line alone. The site's `failures.json` is filled from that table by hand at each release: at the next one the cells are read as "(N failing)". Alternatives: leaving the totals to be counted by the consumer (rejected: the Action shows it can be done, but a reader of the README would still reach for the printed line); "(1 fails)" in the table, by D-239's rule (rejected: a cell is not a sentence). Whether the next release is a patch or a minor one is the owner's at the tag: the field is an addition to the report, and the adapter protocol is unchanged. Tests: `tests/test_report_summary.py`, 12 tests: the totals from stand-ins; the summary of a real run held to the cases of its own report, to the printed count line and to the Action's outputs; the field's place among the earlier ones; the sentence in the three documents; the table's cells. Suite 398 OK (7 skipped); an export's 396 OK (24 skipped); the changed files pass a check against Python 3.9's grammar. No release and no export (owner).

**D-241 (2026-10-04) — The project's identity, recorded in one place: the product name is gpconf, the repository stays gp-omm-conformance, and "the GP/OMM conformance corpus" is the descriptive subtitle; `docs/BRAND.md` holds the name, what it means, the slogan, the sentence in its spoken and written forms, the About line, the headline with its short form, and the order they appear in (owner). Phase 1 of two: nothing public changes, and no README, site page or package file is edited by this entry.** The decisions are the owner's, and `docs/BRAND.md` records them in the owner's words: the name; GP as general perturbations, what CelesTrak and Space-Track call the orbital data, and conf as conformance, whether code reads that data the way it is actually served; the slogan "Test what your parser actually reads."; the sentence, spoken and written; the About line, for a repository's About and a package's summary; the headline "The satellite catalog passed 99,999. Does your software know?", with "Is your code ready?" closing the short form for titles and social posts; and the order where they meet: the headline, then the written sentence, then the install command, with the name's meaning and the slogan lower, on an About page or in an About section. Why one file: the project names and describes itself in more than a dozen places across three repositories, PyPI, Zenodo and the wiki, and they do not say the same thing. The README opens "A conformance corpus for orbital-data parsers", the package's summary reads "Runner for the GP/OMM conformance corpus", the site's description "A free test kit for satellite-data software", and only the site and the package's own name call the product gpconf. From here a surface copies its text from `docs/BRAND.md`, and a change is made there first, with an entry in this log. The file is on the export allowlist and goes public with the next export; until Phase 2 it describes wording that the public surfaces do not all carry yet. One addition to the owner's text, for accuracy: beside the rule that the line beneath the headline explains where the five-digit range ended, the file gives the README's fact, that the catalog's five-digit range ends at 69999 (CelesTrak), so that the line written in Phase 2 does not read as a claim that the catalog's five-digit numbers ran to 99,999 (D-188). The surfaces Phase 2 changes are listed privately with the text each has today: the nine the owner named (the README, PyPI's summary and description, `pyproject.toml`, the repository's About line, `CITATION.cff`, the site's meta description, home page and About page, the profile README) and those a search found beyond them (the Action's name and description and its summary's heading, the runner's help text and the package's docstring, Zenodo's records, the wiki's home page, the site's footer, the site repository's About line and README, the unposted announcement's title). Open for Phase 2, each the owner's: whether `CITATION.cff`'s title changes, which a citation quotes and Zenodo copies; what can move only with a release (PyPI's summary and description, Zenodo's title); the Action's name, which shows in users' workflow logs. Nothing is exported by this entry.
