What D-238 rested on: a message from the owner earlier that day whose text began "The Kelso letter was sent this morning, 2026-10-04 at [time]" and asked for it to be recorded, the bracketed word standing where a time would go, followed by the same sentence with "now" in its place. The session recorded that as an event. It did not see a message, as D-238 says in its own brackets; it read the unfilled "[time]" as a detail to supply instead of a reason to ask; and the "about 07:53 EDT (11:53Z)" in D-238 is the session's clock at the moment it read the word "now", a time no one stated. What was checked for this correction: the session cannot see the owner's mail and so cannot test either statement; no reply from CelesTrak has been logged; nothing else in the records shows a sending. The correction rests on the owner's statement, quoted above, and on nothing inferred. **What is withdrawn.** That the letter was sent, when, and from where. That "from this date CelesTrak's silence is no longer unasked": it is still unasked. That the post announcing the corpus waits for a reply or for a week of silence: no week began, and the post waits for the letter to be sent first, the owner's order of 2026-10-03 unchanged. That the text was "unchanged at sending". **What stands.** The text D-238 summarises exists, as a draft the owner approved on 2026-10-03; its contents are as D-238 lists them. The only contact with CelesTrak's project remains issue #172 (D-204, D-236). **What the draft now needs before it goes**, and why this entry sits where it does: the owner held the v0.6.0 commit for three changes the letter depends on. A user's fetch stops requesting the four known 404s, so the letter's one exception to "stop on any non-200" disappears (D-247), and with it the sentence "no response from CelesTrak is redistributed" needs the one response the corpus then ships. The site's tracker stops its daily Starlink supplemental pull and two weekly requests that answer 404 (site S-083), so the tracker's paragraph changes. And "604 TLE lines" is the wrong unit: they are 604 TLE sets, 1,208 lines of elements (D-248). The letter is redrafted from the released v0.6.0 and sent by the owner. **The rule taken from this.** Something the session cannot observe is recorded as the owner's statement, in the owner's words, with no detail the owner did not give; a message that holds a placeholder is asked about before anything is recorded; and when the owner reports the sending, the entry carries the owner's date and time or none. **Public state.** D-238 was exported in 65ec059 (D-243), so the public log has said since 2026-10-04 that the letter was sent; this entry corrects it at the next export. No other public file says so [tested: a search of the README, the changelog, the documents and the site for the letter and for Dr. Kelso's name finds the data-source credit and nothing else]. The private records that said "sent" are corrected with this entry.

**D-247 (2026-10-04) — One provider response is shipped, as a named exception to D-023: CelesTrak's 16-byte answer `No GP data found`, in `recorded/` with the provenance of every capture of it. A user's fetch no longer requests the four URLs known to answer it, so no request of a user's run is expected to answer anything but 200 (owner's ruling, on a rule question put first).** **The rule question.** D-001 is not touched: it concerns Space-Track bytes and where Alpha-5 element sets come from. D-023 is, by its words: "no raw CelesTrak bytes are shipped", and the body is sixteen raw CelesTrak bytes; the sentence makes no exception for an error message. By its reason it is not: D-019 recorded CelesTrak's silence on redistributing its data as silence and not permission, and the body carries no data, no element, no catalog number, nothing that stands in for a fetch. The same sixteen characters have been in the corpus's public files since v0.1.0, in the runner, which recognises the answer by them, the README, the research notes, the case documents and the expected values [tested: the tag v0.1.0]. The owner's ruling: ship it "under an amended D-023, stated openly as a named exception", and not by reading "provider data" narrowly and saying nothing. D-023 therefore now reads: no raw CelesTrak bytes are shipped but these sixteen. Alternatives: keep requesting the four, the state at 0.5.1, an exception to "stop on any response that is not a 200" that had to be reported to the provider; stop requesting and ship nothing, which takes the empty-answer check (D-143) and six set comparisons out of every user's run; have the runner build the body from the constant it already holds, which ships the same bytes without saying so. **What is shipped.** `recorded/celestrak-no-gp-data-found.txt`, and beside it a provenance file: the five captures of 2026-09-21, which are the four files and the re-capture of one (D-022), with the note that one body was written back by hand from the tool's log (D-013); and the four answers the maintainer's first-time fetch received on 2026-10-04 (D-235); each with its URL, time, HTTP 404, 16 bytes and the same SHA-256 [tested: the manifest, and the metadata that fetch wrote]. `tools/make_recorded.py` writes both and refuses anything but those bytes. **What the fetch does.** The four entries carry `recorded` in the fetch list. No plan requests them, under any flag. A run writes the body to the four paths with metadata that says `provenance: recorded` and `requested: false`, gives the status and the time of the corpus's own capture of that URL, and holds no response header, since no response was received. A file already on disk is left alone, so the maintainer's captures stay captures; a stable-tier one is not copied from an earlier version's cache; and a record whose bytes are not the ones its provenance and the manifest give is refused. `expect_status: 404` is reached now only by the maintainer's re-capture entry. **The figures.** A user's first fetch is 46 requests where it was 50, and 3,131,816 bytes at the snapshot's sizes where it was 3,131,880, with no request expected to answer with an error; on a machine that holds an earlier version's cache, 26 requested, 20 copied and 4 written, where it was 28 and 22 [tested: the suite, on the real list]. The duration is about two minutes [inferred: 50 requests took 2 min 10 s at 0.5.1 (D-235), and four fewer with the pause between them is about ten seconds less; it is measured at the release's timed fetch]. **What a run does.** Where the files are captures, as on the maintainer's copy, nothing changes. Where a file was written from the record, the parser is still handed the body (D-143); the hash item says "the corpus's record of the provider's empty answer … written by the fetch and not requested"; the two checks that compare the TLE answer with the OMM set say "the corpus's record of the TLE answer is 'No GP data found' (HTTP 404, captured …; not requested by this fetch)"; the JSON report lists the files under `recorded`; and a line under the counts says how many there are. A run with no fetched data does not change: the fetch writes the files and the runner does not conjure them, so the offline counts and the Action's stand. Having the runner read the shipped body whenever the file is absent was the alternative, and it would have moved three cases' offline statuses. **What is given up.** A user's run no longer observes CelesTrak giving that answer; it reads the corpus's record of 2026-09-21 and 2026-10-04. For the two live sets a recorded TLE answer is then compared with an OMM set fetched now, and a record can be wrong about today: if the fetched last-30-days group ever holds an id below 100000, CelesTrak's answer that day is data and not the recorded 404. In that case the two checks report `not-exercised` and give the reason; they do not fail, since a stale record is a result neither of the provider nor of the parser. That is a new outcome for an input that did not exist before: for a fetched or captured file the checks behave as they did, and their descriptions in the manifest say what happens with a record. The release stays a minor one on that ground. The site's tracker keeps its daily request for the last-30-days TLE (owner, site S-083), which is now the one place where the answer is still observed. **Fixed with it (owner).** The set comparison quoted `No GP data found` for the SupGP launch-window file, whose body is `No SupGP data found`; it now quotes the text the file holds, which changes one item's wording in the runs that reach it. **Where the exception is named.** Wherever the corpus said that nothing raw is shipped: the README, where the package's contents, "Why no raw files", the fetch's etiquette section and the licence paragraph say it; `NOTICE`, which says the licence does not cover CelesTrak's text; `MANIFEST.md`; `CITATION.cff`'s abstract; the manifest's `design` block, where `provider_data_shipped` is false, `raw_provider_files_shipped`, which held false, lists the one file, and `provider_responses_shipped` describes it; the three case documents. The export allowlist names the two files and the export refuses anything else under `recorded/`; the package carries them, since the installed fetch reads them. The frozen `expected.json` files are untouched. The letter to Dr. Kelso, which has not been sent (D-246), said that no response from CelesTrak is redistributed and described the four requests as its one exception; its next draft says which response is shipped and that the requests are no longer made. Tests: `tests/test_recorded_answer.py`, 22 tests, and the fetch tests that held the old figures; the suite 443 OK.

**D-248 (2026-10-04) — "TLE set" and "line" are used strictly. The two figures the corpus quotes most, 604 and 304, are both counts of sets, and each had been called "lines" in places (owner).** Seen in the draft of the letter to Dr. Kelso, which says "604 TLE lines". Counted from the four derived files [tested]: 604 TLE sets, which are 1,208 element lines, or 1,812 lines with the name line each set has; 257 with the letter A and 347 with the letter T; 603 distinct ids, 270449 being there twice. The 304 are the TLE sets among the maintainer's captures that have the CSV record of the same object beside them, the sample the rendering rules were derived from: 219 analyst, 79 decaying, 2 SupGP and 4 single objects. The renderer reproduces the name line, line 1 and line 2 of each, 912 lines; with the eccentricity rounded it reproduces line 2 of 257, and with the mantissas truncated line 1 of 251 [tested: `tools/validate_render.py`]. Two coincidences, written down so that they are not found again as errors. The maintainer's captures also hold 604 TLE and 2LE sets, in eleven files, re-captures included, all below 100000: the other half of the round-trip test's 1,208. So "604 TLE sets" has two referents, the served and the derived, and a text says which. And a user's fetch brings 304 TLE and 2LE sets, 219, 79 and six single ones: a different 304 from the sample's, since it has the two 2LE sets and lacks the two SupGP ones. **The vocabulary.** A TLE set is one object's lines: line 1 and line 2, with the name line where the format has one. A line is one line. "Record" stays for an OMM record and for what a parser returns, so the runner's item texts do not change. Each figure is said with its noun, and with its lines where a file first states it. Alternatives: "record" for both, which is the word the runner uses for parser output and would blur the two; "element set", which the site's library page uses and which is kept there. **Changed.** The README (the case table, the two sentences on the renderer's validation, the status paragraph); `tools/cases.py` and `tools/make_manifest.py`, and with them `manifest.json`, `MANIFEST.md` and the case documents, regenerated; `docs/WRITERS.md`; the docstring of `gpconf/tle.py`; `CITATION.cff`'s abstract, "derived Alpha-5 TLE sets"; the sentence `tools/crosscheck.py` writes. **Not changed, and why.** `AUDIT.md`, which is the auditor's text. This log, which is append-only: its earlier entries say "lines" and "records" as they were written. `docs/RESEARCH.md` and `docs/PLAN.md`, dated records. `docs/CROSSCHECK.md`, a generated report of 2026-09-21, which takes the corrected sentence when it is next generated. The seven frozen `expected.json` files that embed a copy of the earlier prose (owner: leave them): the runner reads the manifest and none of that prose, as with the check lists of D-143, and a file that holds frozen values is not regenerated for its wording. The derived files' provenance, which already says "TLE records reproduced exactly (lines 0, 1, 2)". The site's About and migration pages and the profile's line follow with the release; the letter's draft is corrected before it is sent (D-246). Test: `tests/test_sets_and_lines.py`, 7 tests: it counts the four derived files and fails on either figure said of lines, or on the shorthand "304/304", in the files the corpus writes today.

**D-249 (2026-10-04) — v0.6.0 prepared again with D-246, D-247 and D-248 in it (owner: the commit was held for them), and the results check run again, since the fetch changed: no status and no count moved, on the maintainer's copy or on a user's data.** D-245 describes the first pass; its figures for the fetch, 50 requests, and 28 requested and 22 copied on a machine with a cache, are those of that tree and are superseded here. Nothing was committed between the two. **What the release now carries.** D-238 to D-248: with what D-245 lists, the correction of D-238 (D-246), the recorded response and the fetch that no longer asks for it (D-247), and the set and line wording (D-248). The constants and the date are as D-245 set them. The README's status paragraph gains two sentences, on the 46 requests and the recorded response, and on "TLE set" and "line"; it no longer says that the printed lines differ from 0.5.1's in wording alone, since the fetch's now differ in substance, and it says "no case and no frozen expected value changed", the descriptions of three checks having gained a sentence on the record. The changelog's 0.6.0 section gains three paragraphs. Regenerated: `recorded/` by `tools/make_recorded.py`; `manifest.json`, `MANIFEST.md` and the case documents, twelve of which change, for the record in the three cases it touches and in the descriptions of the checks, and for the wording in the rest; the three built-in reports and `docs/FAILURES.md`, whose table is unchanged. No `expected.json` changed [tested: the working tree]. **The results check, again.** The same comparison as D-245's, by case status, item status and every count. On the maintainer's copy, where the four files are captures: the thirteen runs of the hand-run libraries and Vallado's C++ against their first-pass reports, and the three built-in adapters against theirs: no status or count differs; one item's text differs in four of the sixteen, the reference adapter, satellite.js and astroz twice, the item that now quotes `No SupGP data found` where it quoted `No GP data found` (D-247), and nothing differs in the other twelve [tested]. On a user's data, which is what D-247 changes: the runner of 0.5.1 as released, on the folder the maintainer's first-time fetch of 2026-10-04 made, against this tree's runner on the same folder with the four files written by this tree's fetch in place of the four it had requested, every request refused while it ran: the reference adapter passes 16 with one case not available and one not exercised, the naive adapter fails 14 and python-sgp4 6, in both; no status or count differs; the item texts that differ are the number agreement of D-239 and the ten items that now say the answer is the corpus's record [tested]. The package as installed does the same: from the trial wheel, a fetch into that folder makes no request and writes the four files from the bundled record, and the reference adapter then passes 16. **Tests.** Under Python 3.14.6 with the Node project of D-245: 443 OK in each of the four environments, with 4, 5, 8 and 7 skipped. An export: 249 files, 5 scrubbed, 0 refused; its suite 441 OK (27 skipped); an offline reference run from it reads 5 cases passing, 11 needing fetched data, one not available and one not exercised, as before. Against the public copy at 65ec059, read without touching it: 46 files differ and 5 are new, the two files of `recorded/`, `tools/make_recorded.py` and two test files; the entries D-243 to D-248 are added and none is edited; no term of the private notes is among the added lines. A trial build: wheel 109 files and sdist 114, two more than the first pass's in each, the record and its provenance; no problem found by the audit; a second build under another umask byte-identical. The trial wheel's dry run of the fetch plans 46 requests and 4 files written from the record, the 50 URLs being 0.5.1's; on the maintainer's machine, whose cache holds 0.5.1, it plans 26 requests, 20 copies and 4 written without `GPCONF_DATA`, and 46 and 4 with it [tested; no request was made]. The run on Python 3.9 and 3.12 is CI's; the changed files pass a check against 3.9's grammar. **Open at this entry.** The duration of a 46-request fetch is inferred until the release's timed fetch. The site states 50 requests on its home page until its entry for this release; its tracker changes separately (site S-083). The public log says the letter was sent (D-238) until this export carries D-246. Nothing is committed, pushed, tagged or uploaded by this entry.

**D-250 (2026-10-04) — v0.6.0 released: checklist steps 10 to 19, each on the owner's word: the commit, the date, the export and CI, the tag, the Action from the scratch repository, the links, the build, the owner's upload, the PyPI check, the owner's timed first-time fetch and the GitHub release; the version DOI is D-251.** Step 10: the preparation, D-245 to D-249, committed privately as one commit. Step 11: the local date at the export was 2026-10-04, the prepared one, so the four date fields stayed; the two "no release carries it yet" cells of the README's table were checked once more at 16:10Z, PyPI's latest sgp4 being 2.27 and npm's latest satellite.js 7.1.0, as at D-245 [tested]. Step 12: the export, 249 files, 5 scrubbed, 0 refused, mirrored into the public clone: 51 files, 5 of them new, the entries D-243 to D-249 added and none edited, no term of the private notes among the added lines; the suite in the clone 441 OK; pushed as public 87fe699 (65ec059..87fe699); CI #63 green on Python 3.9 and 3.12, 0 annotations [tested: GitHub's API]. D-246 has been public since that push. Step 13: the annotated tag v0.6.0, object 8b66a74, on 87fe699, pushed at 16:14:22Z; the run the tag push started, #64, green. The scratch repository's workflow at `@v0.6.0`: run #7 success, the seven jobs green on Ubuntu, macOS and Windows, with the step that reads the report's `summary` and holds it to the Action's outputs passing in each of the six jobs that have a report, the Windows one included, which D-245 had left untested; the one error annotation is the setup-error job's expected exit 2; the summaries on the run's page read "gpconf: preset sgp4", "Corpus 0.6.0, runner gpconf 0.6.0" and "1 needs launch-window data that no fetch brings" [tested: the run's page, and `gh run view`]. Step 14: the files the package description links to, `AUDIT.md` and `docs/ADAPTERS.md` at `blob/v0.6.0/`, with `docs/BRAND.md`, the README, the tree, the recorded response and the raw `pyproject.toml` at the tag, all answer 200 [tested]. Step 15: the tag's export is identical to the pre-push export; with the epoch 1791130360, the tagged commit's committer time, three builds, the tag's export under umask 022 and 077 and the pre-push export before the tag, are byte-identical: wheel 8f32cb59b9c5125a61261331b88b25c0850589ebcc6d26ff9d081eaada9bc718, 109 files, sdist 649bb2837e20f574be833959c3ea3b76e305e6ccfc2a49e90b1fabc3336e3c04, 114 files; the audit finds no problem in either and `twine check` passes (Python 3.14.6, build 1.6.1, setuptools 84.0.0, zlib 1.2.12, macOS arm64); the wheel in a clean environment prints the offline count line and its fetch's dry run plans 46 requests and 4 files written from the record [tested]. Step 16: the owner uploaded. The owner sent the twine command; the session ran it as sent in a Terminal tab, where it stopped at the token prompt, and the owner typed the token. PyPI's upload times are 2026-10-04T16:21:49Z for the wheel and 16:21:51Z for the sdist, and it serves both with the build's hashes [tested: PyPI's JSON]. Step 17: the PyPI page. The summary is the About line of `docs/BRAND.md` and the description opens with the headline, as D-242 ruled for this release; the keywords and twelve classifiers are shown; the description's two links into the repository are at v0.6.0 and none is relative; both files downloaded again through pip's index hash to the build's, the first attempt having met the index's lag, as at 0.4.0; a clean install from PyPI prints `gpconf 0.6.0 | corpus 0.6.0` and "5 pass (exact), 0 pass within tolerance, 0 fail, 0 skip, 11 need fetched data, 1 not available, 1 not exercised" [tested]. Step 18: the owner's timed first-time fetch, from a fresh environment with `pip install gpconf==0.6.0` and `GPCONF_DATA` naming an empty folder, since the maintainer's per-user cache holds 0.5.1's files (D-245): 2 min 2.41 s by `time`; 46 requests, one at a time, every one answered 200; 4 files written from the corpus's record, with no request; nothing stopped the run and no unexpected response was kept; 3,200,451 bytes downloaded, 3,200,515 on disk with the four; exit status 0; 26 files matching the snapshot, 22 stable and 4 live, no stable source drifted, 24 live sources differing. The reference adapter on that folder then passes 16 cases, with one not available and one not exercised. That settles what D-247 left inferred: a first fetch of 46 requests takes about two minutes, seven seconds less than 0.5.1's 50. Step 19: the GitHub release "v0.6.0 — a fetch that asks for no known error", id 403106405, published 2026-10-04T16:30:30Z on the existing tag, Latest, not a pre-release, its body the draft the owner read and cut by one paragraph, stored identical to it [tested: GitHub's API].

**D-251 (2026-10-04) — v0.6.0 version DOI 10.5281/zenodo.23140585 (Zenodo record 23140585, created 2026-10-04T16:30:35Z) set in the slots prepared for it: a ninth `identifiers` entry in `CITATION.cff`, the version-DOI sentence of the README, and the 0.6.0 changelog paragraph that had promised it. The record carries the cited title of `docs/BRAND.md`, the first to do so.** Same sequence as D-237. The record appeared five seconds after the release: version v0.6.0, `isSupplementTo` `tree/v0.6.0`, the archive of the tag 1,268,454 bytes, licence MIT, open access; DataCite registered the DOI at 16:30:36Z; the version DOI resolves to the record, and the concept DOI, unchanged, to the same record [tested: Zenodo's API, DataCite's API and doi.org]. Its title is "gpconf: a conformance corpus for orbital-data parsers crossing the five-digit catalog-number boundary", taken from `CITATION.cff` at the tag: D-242's ruling that Zenodo's title changes at this release held, and the seven earlier records keep the title they were published with [tested: the two newest records]. The earlier version DOIs are kept, v0.1.0's still named as the release the independent audit covered. The PyPI description of 0.6.0 keeps the README as it was at the tag, slot sentence included, as for the earlier releases. The GitHub release's line "Version DOI: added here once Zenodo mints it" is edited to carry the DOI, the stored body identical to the draft with that line changed and the release still Latest. The tag is not moved: the DOI lives on `main` after the tag; the site gets the same DOI under its own entry.

**D-252 (2026-10-04) — For the next release, not released (owner instruction): the fetch's `User-Agent` names gpconf, the version that asks and the repository, and the README's size for a user's fetch is the measured 3.2 MB where it said 3.1.** **The header.** Every request of `gpconf fetch` has carried `gp-omm-conformance-corpus/0.1 (fixture fetch, each URL once; see repository README)` since the corpus was built: a name that is not the kit's (D-241), a version that was never the corpus's, and a pointer to a README with no address. It was found on 2026-10-04 while the kit's description of its own traffic was checked against the released code: the 46 requests of the owner's timed fetch at the 0.6.0 release all carried that string [tested: the `request_headers` of the fifty metadata files of that fetch], and nothing in it would let CelesTrak's operator tell from a log what software was asking or which release. It now reads `gpconf/<version> (+https://github.com/hneogy/gp-omm-conformance; fetch, each URL once)`, the version taken from `gpconf.__version__`, so it moves with the release and cannot be forgotten at one. D-007 stands: the header says nothing about the person running the fetch; `GPCONF_CONTACT` appends a contact for whoever chooses to, and `GPCONF_USER_AGENT` replaces the string, both as before. The README's "Fetching responsibly" gives the header and the two variables, which the README had not named (D-007 names one of them). Alternatives: the product name without the version (rejected: the version is what tells an operator whether a release that changed the fetch has reached a user); the site's address in place of the repository's (rejected: the repository holds the fetch list and the issue tracker a stopped run points at, and the site's tracker already sends the site's address under its own header). What does not change: the metadata of the corpus's own captures, which record the header they were made with; the fetch list; any case, check or expected value. A file fetched from now on records the new header in its `.meta.json`, which no check reads. **The size.** The README said "46 requests, 3.1 MB" twice in the quick start and once under "Fetching responsibly". 3.1 MB is the sum of the sizes of the corpus's own captures of those 46 files, 3,131,816 bytes (D-247; 3,131,880 at 0.5.1 with the four requests since dropped, D-231). The owner's timed first-time fetch at the 0.6.0 release downloaded 3,200,451 bytes (D-250), and the one at the 0.5.1 release left 3,201,685 bytes on disk (D-235). The 68,635 bytes between the captures and the 0.6.0 fetch are live data: sixteen of the 46 files differ in size, and nearly all of the difference is the analyst group, larger now in each of its five formats, and the decaying list [tested: the size of each of the 46 files in the maintainer's captures and in the metadata of that fetch]. The site and the release notes of both versions say 3.2 MB; the README now does too. The 3.1 was not a slip: `tests/test_fetch_text.py` held the README's figure to the manifest's sizes, under D-230's rule that the section's figures cannot drift from the fetch list. A user does not download the captures, though, and the test now holds the README to the measured 3,200,451 bytes, with the captures' sum beside it in the same sentence ("where the corpus's own captures of the same files sum to 3.1 MB; the live files change size") and a check that the two stay within a twentieth of each other, so that a fetch list that changes materially fails the test until the fetch is timed again. The timed fetch comes after the upload, so a release's README carries the measurement of the release before it and says which. The changelog's sections for 0.5.1 and 0.6.0 keep "3.1 MB", as published; the Unreleased section says why the README differs from them. Tests: the changed test, and `tests/test_fetch_user_agent.py`, six tests: the default string against the version constant, no contact unless given, the replacement, the header a stubbed request really sends and the metadata records, the README's sentence, and one size in the README's three places. No version constant moves and nothing is exported: this rides with the next release, on the owner's word.

**D-253 (2026-10-04) — `docs/RESEARCH.md`, section 3, gains the usage policy's paragraph on NAT as the owner's account of the page, not as a quotation (owner instruction).** The owner's instruction, 2026-10-04: add "the usage policy's NAT paragraph — he has no way to know how many users reach CelesTrak through one IP address — read from the page as of 2026-10-03". The research record's rule is verbatim quotations with URLs and retrieval dates, and the paragraph's words are not available to a session: no session requests celestrak.org (standing rule), the one session reading of that page, on 2026-09-21, was made for redistribution terms and quoted nothing on this point (section 11.1), and no earlier record of the project holds the text [tested: a search of `docs/`, `DECISIONS.md` and the session's transcript for the paragraph]. So the entry gives what the owner said, in the owner's words, says in its heading that it is an account and not a quotation, and says that until the paragraph is quoted with the page's "Updated" date nothing cites it as CelesTrak's wording. This is D-246's rule applied to a page: what a session did not see is recorded as a statement, with nothing added. One sentence of the entry is this record's own reading and is marked [inferred]: that a hosted CI runner's address is such a shared address and that the per-address limits therefore count its users together. Alternatives: fetch the page (rejected: the standing rule, and a fetch today would not show the page as of 2026-10-03); write the paragraph as a quotation from the owner's summary (rejected: it would put words in CelesTrak's mouth); leave RESEARCH.md alone until the text is at hand (rejected: the owner asked for the entry, and the account, labelled as one, is true as far as it goes). Open: the paragraph's own words, to replace the account when the owner supplies them; listed under the handoff's open questions, since a sentence written to CelesTrak's operator may rest on it. Not exported; it rides with the next export, on the owner's word.

**D-254 (2026-10-04) — Follow-up to D-253: in `docs/RESEARCH.md`, section 3, the owner's account of the usage policy's NAT paragraph is replaced by the paragraph itself, as the owner read it on the page on 2026-10-04 and supplied it (owner instruction).** D-253 recorded the paragraph as an account because no session had its words, and left them open. The owner has now given them: two sentences of https://celestrak.org/usage-policy.php, the page dated "2026 May 15, Updated 2026 May 22", "as I read it on 2026-10-04". Section 3 quotes them exactly as given [tested: the blockquote, unwrapped, equals the supplied text character for character, 506 characters]. the `sgp4` extra pins `sgp4>=2.27`, python-sgp4 having dropped 3.9 at 2.26, so Python 3.9 refuses the extra loudly instead of resolving to 2.25, and the README says so where it names the preset; CI declares `permissions: contents: read`, pins `actions/checkout` and `actions/setup-python` by commit id, and adds a `windows-latest` job at Python 3.12 whose first run comes with the next public push [untested until then]; **D-270 (2026-10-09) — The Windows CI job's first run failed 42 of 445 tests (public run 37930807769 on 662da73, 33 failures and 9 errors), every one a portability assumption of the suite or the tooling and none a wrong expected value; fixed and the suite made genuinely Windows-clean rather than skipped, under the owner's gate "if the new Windows job fails, stop and fix it before tagging".** Three causes, from the job's log read whole. **One, line endings:** the repository had no `.gitattributes`, so git on Windows converted text files at checkout; a corpus whose expected values and manifest hash the files as they are cannot allow any checkout to rewrite them, and the conversion cascaded into the stable-source-drift checks, the export comparisons and everything downstream. A `.gitattributes` of one rule, `* -text`, now ships, allowlisted for export. **Two, shell quoting and the interpreter's name:** command lines handed to the platform shell were quoted with `shlex.quote`, whose POSIX single quotes `cmd.exe` rejects, and the fetch hint named `python3`, a name Windows installs do not have. `fetch_hint` now says `python` and quotes with `subprocess.list2cmdline` on Windows (`python3` and `shlex.quote` elsewhere), and the six test modules that build command lines for `CommandParser` quote the same way; `tests/test_not_fetched.py`'s expectations state both forms. **Three, path separators:** `tools/export_public.py` matched manifest paths against the allowlist with native separators (normalised to `/` now), and `locate.user_cache_dir` joined a faked platform's path with the host's separator — it now answers each faked platform with that platform's own joiner (`ntpath`/`posixpath`), and the win32 test expects `ntpath`. **Alternatives rejected:** skipping the failing tests on Windows (a green job must mean the suite passes, not that it looks away), and documenting `core.autocrlf=false` in the README (the repository can enforce byte-exactness itself). **Also set right in passing:** the 0.6.2 changelog entry's sentence that the Windows job's first run "comes with the next public push" (it has now run, and the entry tells what it found), and its two D-262 sentences, which contradicted each other after the adapters' comments changed — the entry now says the comments change with this release's build. The suite on macOS after the fix: 454 tests, OK, seven skipped. The public export, push and CI re-run follow under the owner's standing instruction for this release; the tag waits for the Windows job to pass.

**D-271 (2026-10-09) — The second Windows run (37933368138 on 4d175f7) came down from 42 failures to 15, and every one of the 15 is in the tests' own assumptions, none in the package; fixed in a second round, still under the owner's gate, with no test skipped as a whole.** **The seven that share one cause:** every test that drives the composite Action does it through `run_step`, which ran `bash -c` by name — and on a Windows runner a bare `bash` can resolve to `C:\Windows\System32\bash.exe`, the WSL doorway, which without a distribution prints a notice and exits 1 having run nothing. That fits the symptom exactly (exit 1, no summary written, both runs identical), and is recorded [inferred] from the symptom until the next run proves it: `run_step` now resolves the bash that `shell: bash` means — on Windows the one Git carries, found from `git`'s own location — and, when a step writes no summary, hands the step's output to the assertion message instead of silence, which is what cost this diagnosis a CI round-trip. **The six expectation fixes:** two `relpath` comparisons against `/`-separated literals normalised (`tests/test_export.py`, `tests/test_harnesses.py`'s `files_under`); the `data_root` test's POSIX-absolute inputs now expected through `os.path.abspath`, which anchors a rootless path to the drive on Windows, as the function answers; and the remaining `python3`-and-POSIX-quote expectations in `tests/test_install_layout.py`, `tests/test_fetch_optin.py` and `tests/test_action.py` state the platform's interpreter and quoting, the shadowed assertions behind the first failures included. **The one platform truth:** `tests/test_stage_package.py`'s staged-tree assertion held the tree's POSIX modes, which NTFS cannot carry; the epoch stamp is now asserted on every platform and the mode halves on POSIX alone, which looks away from nothing, because the archive writers never read modes from the tree — `repack_wheel` and `repack_sdist` set 0644 and 0755 themselves (`tools/stage_package.py` lines 200–228, read for this entry) and `test_repacked_archives_depend_on_their_contents_alone` passed on Windows in both runs. The suite on macOS: 454 tests, OK, seven skipped.

**D-272 (2026-10-09) — The third Windows run (37934867845 on 62bf2b3) settled D-271's [inferred]: with Git's bash resolved, every Action test passed, so the bare `bash` had indeed been System32's WSL doorway. One failure remained, again the test's own expectation: `test_data_root_order`'s last assertion held the cache path to the POSIX tail `gpconf/<version>`, and Windows's own layout is `gpconf\Cache\<version>` — the assertion now states the running platform's tail. Nothing else failed: 451 of 452 passed on Windows, the line-ending, quoting, separator and bash fixes all holding.

Only then the tag. **The tag:** annotated v0.6.2, "gp-omm-conformance 0.6.2", object d77201e on 3302fae, tagger time 2026-10-09T13:16:28Z, pushed; its CI run 37935680738 green; the six URLs the package description points at answer 200 at the tag and the raw `pyproject.toml` there says 0.6.2. **The expiring claims, re-checked at 13:16Z before the tag** [tested: PyPI's and npm's JSON, GitHub's API]: sgp4's latest is 2.27 of 2026-07-03, satellite.js's 7.1.0 of 2026-07-23, Gpredict's latest release v2.6 of 2026-08-16, tle.js's v5.0.3 of 2026-07-21 — every "in no release yet" cell of the README's table still true on release day. **The scratch repository:** both workflows' `@v0.6.1` lines moved to `@v0.6.2` in `gpconf-action-test.yml` alone (the reproducible-build workflow stays at v0.6.1 until PyPI serves 0.6.2, since it compares against PyPI); the tenth run, 37935928340, seven jobs green; the header's counts verified first by local runs of the Action's script from the release export (reference 0 failed of 5 exercised, naive 5 of 5, python-sgp4 3 of 4; summaries "Corpus 0.6.2, runner gpconf 0.6.2"). **The build:** the tag's export (`git archive v0.6.2`) byte-identical to the pre-push export; epoch 1791551667 (3302fae's committer time); three builds in the throwaway environment (build 1.6.1, setuptools 84.0.0, twine 7.0.0, macOS arm64) — the tag's export under umask 022 and 077 and the pre-push export under 022 — all byte-identical: wheel 67664323b03c6cfa24e57c578a621bf05a3548edbcd984dedd45c10644024305 (109 files), sdist b651fe665a0a154af810f943e907ad31484b7762e2320f758d2071c79f3c17be (114 files); the audit 0 problems for each; `twine check` passed for both. The wheel installed with `--no-index` into a clean environment with an empty home prints `gpconf 0.6.2 | corpus 0.6.2` and "18 cases: 5 pass (exact), 0 pass within tolerance, 0 fail, 0 skip, 11 need fetched data, 1 not available, 1 not exercised"; its `gpconf fetch --dry-run` plans 46 requests and 4 files written from the record; nothing was created in the home folder. The files, checked with `shasum -c`, are in `Code Files/gp-omm-conformance/gpconf-0.6.2/`. **As the owner asked, on the record: `tools/verify_against_spacetrack.py` was edited in this release (the UTF-8 sweep of D-269) but not run.** No request went to celestrak.org or space-track.org in any of this release's steps; `gpconf fetch` ran only as `--dry-run`. **Still the owner's, in order:** the upload with the owner's own credential, the PyPI check, the Zenodo version DOI into `CITATION.cff`, the README and the changelog, the GitHub release, and the site's version constants (an S-entry of its own).

**D-274 (2026-10-09) — Dr. Kelso replied to the letter of 2026-10-05; the reply is recorded here word for word, as the letter promised ("A correction from you goes into the project's public record word for word"). Received Thu 8 Oct 2026, 23:59 EDT, from ts.kelso@celestrak.org; the owner pasted it into the record on 2026-10-09.** The reply, exactly as received:

> Dear Honorius,
>
> On the surface, it seems like you should have have a problem.
> Your description suggests you have read through the GP
> documentation (https://celestrak.org/NORAD/documentation/gp-data-formats.php)
> and our usage policy (https://celestrak.org/usage-policy.php).
> You even catch that the GROUP=last-30-days&FORMAT=tle will have
> no data, since all objects cataloged in the last 30 days has a
> catalog number of 100000+.
>
> The only thing I will point out is that we have no data above
> 69999 other than the analyst satellites in 80000-89999. And the
> Alpha-5 decoder in Dave Vallado's code does not change
> CelesTrak's position that we do not support Alpha-5 in any way,
> due to the reasons cited in our documentation's FAQs.

Nothing in the reply contradicts a recorded byte. **What it occasions, each ruled by the owner on 2026-10-09:** the sentences that say "above 99999" of CelesTrak's TLE format tighten to his statement; the CSV, JSON and tracker claims stand as measured; and three wordings change on the owner's approval of the shown diffs — the profile introduction attributes Alpha-5 to Space-Track rather than "provider data", the #172/#174 reports are said to be against Dave Vallado's reference code, hosted in CelesTrak's repository, and the manifest's "CelesTrak-style renderings" becomes "rendered with CelesTrak's eccentricity truncation".
